ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e418	As the President has explained, this debate on the communication of research and the examination of several other issues by the Church, Education and Research Committee. I will concentrate my dossier on the research report, and not so much on Document No. The eight proposals, which I am sure will be taken care of in later speeches in today's debate. When I stand here, it is primarily as a party representative. I still allow myself to start a little personal. I want to say that I have been at Stortinget for eight years, and this has been one of the most rewarding work I have participated in. Vice-Chancellors, Deans, professors and organizations have actively contributed to the best possible way of dealing with this matter by Storting. For my part, it has been almost a journey of formation to lead this process. Now this education journey has mainly taken place within Ring 3, but equally full. Then I would also like to thank my colleagues in the committee for allowing me to direct this work, but also for what I have experienced has been very constructive cooperation across the parties, both internally in the block and to a large extent between the blocks. I think that's good. I would also like to thank the Ministry of Knowledge for both informative answers and for some of the responses to the requests sent by the Committee. The committee is behind much of the option. For me and for the Progress Party, it has been an objective in itself: to get the widest possible majority behind the main lines of Norwegian research policy. I feel that there has to a large extent been the review tone in the other parties as well. Whether we're talking about universities, colleges, institutes or others that are involved in research, for example. in business, predictability is very much needed – predictability regardless of who wins the autumn elections. I'm sure the right-hand side will do that. But we have to have predictability that goes beyond the four-year period. We cannot leave to universities, colleges, institutes and businesses to plan strategic knowledge work based on four-year horizons. We have to facilitate a longer horizon than that. I believe that the Committee's work has contributed to this. We have a broad political anchoring too much of the main lines of the Communication and also, in fact, a relatively broad political anchoring beyond the content of the Government Communication. We have a whole seven proposals which will be adopted unanimously today. Overall, the Committee has spent a lot of time in four areas. Others are given an explanation of their weight. I have focused on the internationalisation of the Norwegian Academy in general and research in particular, the debate on relevance and/or quality of work and academics, the need for a pooling of human and financial resources around environments that can bring Norwegian research into the division it should be, and research infrastructure. We do not agree on everything, but we largely agree on the description of reality, and also to some extent on the direction in which the instruments should be set up in the future. On internationalization, it is to say that we are a small and sparsely populated country on the world's absolute edge, on the other side of the world of where people live most. Therefore, participation in international cooperation on research of fundamental importance is for Norway. We need to do that to follow international research fronts – but also to do our job of helping to move them. We need to do this to recruit talent from abroad to Norwegian research and education institutions. We need to do this in order to strengthen Norwegian business and manage international networks, and we simply need to participate in international research cooperation to ensure the quality of what we are doing in Norway. We must do this by exposing this – to colleagues abroad. Academia in Norway has a long and strong tradition of interaction with the Pangerman and Anglo-Americans in the field of culture. If we take Japan into that category, we can call it the comfort zone of the Norwegian Academy – with long traditions. We get it done, and we've done it for a long time. The institutions that have succeeded in interacting with German, British and Japanese institutions have been called internationalised. It can't be like that in the future. We need to maintain and strengthen the relationships we have with these countries, obviously, but we need to be outside the comfort zone in the future, among other things. to establish interaction with the countries that are subsequently called the BRICS countries: Brazil, Russia, India, China and South Africa. These are rapidly growing economies that are increasingly important for research internationally and increasingly for the world economy. I hope that both the Ministry of Knowledge, the Ministry of Foreign Affairs and the Ministry of Economic Affairs and Trade will read the statement, stating that the Committee will see it as an advantage if positions were created dedicated to research and innovation cooperation at the Norwegian embassies in Brazil and South Africa. We don't have those today. Internationalisation must begin somewhere. Let me go into the PhD system a little bit. It is a strength – I think everyone agrees – that many foreigners come to Norway and help raise the quality of the programmes we have in Norway. In terms of marine technology and petroleum technology, we are totally dependent on foreign citizens. It is hardly lucky that we are making some disciplines in Norway entirely dependent on migration. But it is good that Norway and Norwegian research systems are attractive to foreigners. It's obvious that it enhances the quality of what we're doing. When the NIFU report of 2012, with a doctorate in the workplace, was published in winter, it was a big debate that it is a problem that so many people go home. I agree. It is a problem that so many of the foreign nationals who come to Norway leave the country immediately after they have taken their PhD. Here. When the debate went on about this report in winter, it seemed as if it were almost just Tord Lien and the Progress Party who were concerned that so few nordics were going out. Only 7 pst. by the Nordics, after having completed their doctor's degree, they leave. in Norway. It does not contribute to the internationalisation of Norwegian research. That's why I'm glad that a joint committee agrees that it must be an objective that more nordics go out after graduating from PhD. I am pleased that what is today the minority, but that until the fall will be the majority in the Grand Duchy – Right, Christian People’s Party, Left and the Advance Party – promotes proposals to establish incentive schemes to get more Norwegians to leave after graduating from PhD. It will strengthen the internationalisation of Norwegian research. The 7th EU Framework Programme for Research is in the process of being phased out. It shall be replaced by a new and somewhat different programme, which shall also include innovation, namely the EU Horizon 2020. When the Communication was presented, the government chose to spread uncertainty about Norwegian participation in the next EU research programme, despite the fact that the Communication is full of praise for the importance of Norwegian research on participation in EU Framework Programmes. The government also says that Horizon 2020 will be even more adapted to the Norwegian research and education environments. It is therefore very gratifying that a unanimous committee clearly states that Norway should participate in Horizon 2020. The alternative would have been that the major Norwegian research universities had to interact with the European B team in terms of research, and that the vast majority of universities had been excluded from participating in European research cooperation. The fact that a unanimous committee today says that we are joining in is very good. At the same time, it is important to maintain the balance between adaptation to the EU and to strengthen research fields of national importance. It is an objective to maximise the quota for the various EU research programmes, but it must not be an overarching objective. We're already participating in programs that have a cost of twelve times the quota. Norwegian researchers participate in EU research programmes with a price of 12 times the Norwegian quota. We get a lot left for European research cooperation regardless of whether we get heads for heads get everything back to Norway. It is also important to bet where we have a knowledgeable, natural or nutritional advantage, such as: in the field of cancer, where we have competences, in the field of arctic, where we have natural advantages, and in the case of energy, where we have knowledgeable, natural and not least nutritional advantages. I want to praise the government in an area that combines these things – increasing efforts on European research and EU programmes and focusing on what we are good at. The efforts of the Ministry of Fisheries and Coastal Affairs and other departments have resulted in the establishment of the Joint Programming Initiatives (Joint Programming Initiatives) under the auspices of the EU. Then you combine both, you make the EU bet on something we're good at. We have to do more of that. In this international context, we must also see the Committee's desire for progress in the establishment of the Global Centres of Expertise. It is a mechanism that stimulates interaction between enterprises and academies with strong international positions, where companies and academia can play together well in Norway, and play on each other's networks outside. Although Trond Giske does not have the habit of appearing in the staging room when we discuss research, I hope that he reads the position at least, where it is clear and clear that a unanimous church, education and research committee assumes that there must be ten years of funding horizon also on the Global Centres of Expertise, and that any plans for a more short-term funding horizon will be abandoned. To establish structures for interaction between academia and business, the time horizon must be of a certain size. The NCEs have had five plus five, it should also have the Global Centres of Expertise. A major or minor concern is the debate and balance between relevance and quality. I want to start by stressing, as it stands in the setting as well, that there is no relevance without quality. It cannot be said that education and research are relevant to anything, unless they are also of high quality. The aim must be to strengthen the relevance of working life without jeopardising quality. The Academy has several roles to play: it will create the foundation for a cultural nation, create the opening up of common universal values, and also contribute to a competent and forward-looking working life. A one-sided focus on relevance to work today will cause some loss. Candidates who can do the same as today's employees cannot challenge the business they are going to be involved in. We're going to do research on what business is doing, it's often said. No, we're not going to do that. We're going to be researching what business is going to do in the future, in 15 years, 30 years, 45 years – we're going to do that. Some of these debates on research often end up dealing with New Public Management, so I want to be clear that all the parties in the Norwegian pothole in this setting are behind the counterside system, New Public Management, as the overall management system. But all the parties, at least almost, at least the Progress Party, are concerned that the counting edge system also means that not everything is going to be completely optimal. If you're just going to do research on what you know to prove, if you're just going to do research on hypothesis, you know that you can prove, something's going down the road. I have a lot of pages left in my script, but I'm not going to get it, so I'm going to get back to my other considerations about these questions later. I have now explained the position and I am addressing the proposals on which the Progress Party is involved.

Norway is well placed to become a country for excellent research. The stories about areas that we've made up our minds are for fascination and enthusiasm. It's only nice to hear Professor Stenseth tell you about how we've brought salmon from being willing to become a domestic animal, through basic research. Norway is in the front line. It's fascinating to see how the Moser sect has built up a strong research environment and discovered the grid cells, which have a key function in the brain's work to give us a sense of place. Norwegian researchers are at the forefront of solving the mysteries that are going on in our brain. And not least, it's nice to read about the development of multi-phase technology. It has made it possible to reduce dramatically the production costs of oil and also to open up oil fields that would otherwise not be propelling. This and a lot of other examples show that we can be in the front line, that we have good results, that we have reason to be proud of our universities and colleges, and of the business research that is going on. There has been a lot of research under the red-green government. As of 2005, research appropriations have grown by around 6.7 billion. kr, or whole 32 pst. Under the red-green government, research and development efforts in the state budget have increased from 0.8 pst. of GDP in 2005 to 0,92 pst. of GDP in 2013. This has produced results to the extent that research can be quantitatively measured. Of the countries compared in the research survey, Norway has seen the highest growth in the number of articles published. The number of articles published has increased by 84 pst. from 2001 to 2011. In the case of quotations, we are above the EU-15 average. But we can get better. We want to give predictability to research and to confirm the high ambitions of the sector. Norway shall have a well-functioning research system, a high quality of research, a high level of internationalisation in research and an effective exploitation of research resources and results. Once the Committee has politically ended up dealing with the main features of the communication, it shows just high ambitions. We'll bet together on the knowledge society to stand ready to meet tomorrow's challenges. We will focus on predictability and long-termity for our researchers and research communities. We are united behind most of the tools that are needed to get good research in this country. We depend on research and higher education efforts to be long-term, coordinated and clearly prioritised. A 10-year long-term national research plan shall be established. The aim is to ensure predictability and improve quality. At the same time, the long-term plan will provide for innovative and cross-border research that can give us a new recognition in the long term. It shall include priorities for research and higher education and shall provide leadership for investment from a 10-year perspective. It concerns construction, research infrastructure, scholarships and study places. Such a plan has been sought by the sector, and it is a pleasure here to also signify the firm efforts of the Council of State to put such a plan into place. Other measures now being queried are the introduction of a test scheme for entry positions for particularly talented, younger researchers. A system for several doctoral degrees in public enterprises, similar to that currently available for doctoral degrees in cooperation with industry, shall be investigated. Research results shall be made available and disseminated in a language that facilitates the use of knowledge by users. The use of temporary units shall be reduced. More efforts will be made to link research and education, including: through student-active research. An assessment shall assess whether the overall funding of universities and higher education institutions is appropriate to stimulate good quality and to promote institutions in disciplines where they have special advantages. We got important and important recordings in the consultation round. There was a desire for more focus on the nutritional part of research, because there's no doubt that this is where we have a challenge. The public share of research efforts is high. But in business, too little is invested if we're to achieve the goal of matching 3 pst. GDP. The state cannot take responsibility for research into business, but the state can take responsibility for the fact that there are incentives at the bottom that make business more researchable. We are therefore pleased to be able to show that the TaxFUNN scheme was significantly improved in proposals for a revised State budget. We refer to the text that focuses on the BIA system and that there is agreement on how good the GCE system is. Research policy shall focus on five strategic objectives, where research shall contribute to meeting global challenges, in particular in the fields of environment, climate, sea, food security and energy. It's all agreed, except for the Right. It shall further contribute to good health, less social health differences and high-quality health and care services, as well as research-based welfare policies and the exercise of professional skills in welfare services, and to a knowledge-based business life throughout the country, as well as to the development of businesses in the fields of food, marine, maritime, travel, energy, environment, biotechnology, ICT and new materials. We think these objectives are important, and we have some depths: Norway is facing major challenges. There are increasing numbers of older people in this country. Where there were four workers behind each pensioner around 1970, there will be about two in 2030. This affects the generation accounts. These accounts are the glues of society. It's assumed that we get from the state when we're young, that we pay into the community when we have strong back and can work, and that we get back when we become seniors. These accounts must be aggregated. If we are to fund the welfare society of our children in the future, we must make sure that we meet the demography with research and technological development. To meet this challenge, the Committee will focus on dementia and ageing research in health and welfare research. The climate challenges we are facing are undisputable. The majority have stressed the importance of the high ambitions Norway has for environmental research. Research into working life is also important and has been placed in the Committee's comments. Petroleum research is not part of the new EU programme Horizon 2020. It has been particularly necessary to focus on this research in Norway. These areas reflect, in particular, key societal and business challenges in our country, and they are close to international spheres of activity. We have had a thorough real-life examination of the research communication in the committee. That means that we have made some suggestions on other points we think are necessary to have a good research policy in the country. It's a majority to: present a report on the quality of studies in higher education stimulates increased competition in order to contribute to higher quality in Norwegian research, including strengthening the open programmes of the Research Council, in particular in the field of basic research stimulating more research in industry and for stronger interaction between academia and industry, focus more on international excellence in research environments and university environments at the global summit, following the model of the GCEs. To further enhance the quality of Norwegian research and higher education, international cooperation needs to be more closely integrated into the everyday lives of several Norwegian researchers. There is agreement to contribute to the further internationalisation of Norwegian research through association with the European Research Area ERA, full participation in Horizon 2020 and the development of programme cooperation with leading research nations. There is a majority in favour of establishing an electoral research programme and there is agreement to propose to increase investment in advanced scientific equipment and other research infrastructures to allow Norwegian researchers access to world-class equipment. We're happy about that. We have wanted a consensus behind the main lines of research policy, and I would say that we have achieved, with some important exceptions that we will probably get closer to.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e657	We are probably more members of the Church, Education and Research Committee who have looked forward to the debate here today, and I would also like to take the opportunity, on behalf of the Right, to thank Mr Tord Lien for a very good work on the Research Communication. I think it is time for research policy to be recognised as perhaps the most strategic policy area we have today. When talking about strategic policies in the past, it may have been primarily defence and security policy that pointed out itself as the important policy area for – let's say – a country's destiny and the future of a country. I believe that there is reason to say that research policy – at least knowledge policy in general – has taken on this role. That's the overall policy we're talking about. It is a cross-sectoral policy, and it is perhaps the policy area that is most important for the future of a country. It's about developing the knowledge nation, it's about securing the future, and it's about having a readiness to know that it's going well in the country over the years to come. To a very large extent, research policy has to do with a very long-term perspective. Of course, the government has also taken this into account in the research communication – not least in the title of the communication. Worldwide, we see massive investment in research, the development of new knowledge and new technologies. In many ways it can be said that the international arms race today has been replaced by a knowledge race. And it's interesting to note that countries that have had or still have demanding security challenges, for example. Three countries, such as Israel, Singapore and South Korea, are also among the countries in the world that are all committed, most of all to research and technology development. All three countries I mentioned have a bet of about. 5 pst. GDP. In Norway, therefore, we are discussing whether to have a 3 pst. target, which most developed countries have, and which is also recommended by the European Union, and we know that we are far below this target today. In addition to the countries I mentioned, this applies to several other countries, and it is tempting to draw out our Nordic neighbouring countries as well. In Sweden, the investment in research is almost 4 pst. of GDP, and Swedish GDP is not much smaller than Norwegian. I think Sweden may never have forgiven us that Norway went past Sweden in terms of GDP a few years ago, but it is not that big a difference. What is the big difference, if we compare Norway with Sweden, is that Swedish business accounts for a significant share of the national research effort. As far as I remember, Swedish business researches today for approx. 2.6 pst. of Swedish GDP, while Norwegian industry is researching for approx. 0.7 pst. – maybe a little more – of Norwegian GDP. So there's a big difference between Norwegian and Swedish business. At the same time, both Norway and Sweden are relatively small countries. Norway is probably even smaller and, to a greater extent, a foreign country, and for a country such as Norway, positioning in this field is becoming important. If research policy is considered an important strategic policy area, it is very important for Norway how we think and how we plan to position ourselves. What will be our contribution to solving the world's major challenges? What do we need, first of all, out of knowledge? And how does Norway become more attractive as a co-operative country? We are seeing increasing international cooperation in this area. In the field of trade, it is said that the experts operate with three success criteria: location, location, location. I am tempted to say that for research the three main factors must be: quality, quality, quality. In a country that is highly dependent on one sector, both directly and indirectly purely economically, both in business and in government budgets, it is important to ensure high-quality research in selected areas of major importance for Norway and to ensure technology and knowledge transfer from the large sector today — oil and gas — to other industries and industries. It may be tempting to bring forward the marine sector where Norway has a strong advantage both in terms of business and competence. Marine sector, which in turn is highly dependent on marine research and climate research, has a relatively new research area called marine bioprospection, etc. It has great implications and offers great opportunities for food production, medical production, and thus also for addressing the major global challenges in food and health, for example. The potential is enormous. We could make an even stronger bet on this field. As mountain dwellers, I may also have to mention that there are strong ambitions in Bergen, and there is a strategic effort to co-locate and strengthen the relevant environments in Bergen in climate research, marine research and marine research. This will be important for Bergen's future. Then we have had a discussion already in this debate on the so-called 3 pst. goal. Why do you have such a goal? Why is it recommended internationally? There is a measure of how much of society’s value creation today should be set aside and invested to provide the basis for tomorrow’s value creation. It is a similar objective as the one recommended for Norwegian municipalities, to have an operating result of 3 pst. – it’s actually there, incidentally – and set it aside to secure the economy and investment for the future. So if we look at the 3pst measure, and if we look at the world, it's not a 3pst measure in itself crazy ambitious. We have countries operating with both 4 pst. targets and 5 pst. targets. So the government doesn't want to fix this goal. In the right, we have had a debate on this – also in front of the National Meeting of the Right. We've chosen to set this goal to 2030. I know that the Left and Christian People's Party operates with a more ambitious objective. The point is at least that once you have fixed such a target, you can sit down and plan how the goal is to be achieved. If you don't set the target, such planning is completely meaningless. For this reason, the government will not even exit the take-off pit when it comes to setting ambitious research action plans in Norway for the years to come. We have a time horizon to 2030, it's four major periods. It is then possible to sit down and break this down and make operational plans for how to achieve such a goal. And of course, as several speakers have already said, the big challenge lies in business. If business needs to research for 2 pst. of GDP — and this is also agreed by the government, because it operates with this target, but without a time-limit — means that it is threefold compared to the current level. This will require strong public instruments – the Research Council said in its input to the Research Communication – and there are such instruments we need to discuss how to design and strengthen. Then it is quite striking – I have to say it at the end – that the Minister of Economic Affairs does not seem to show up here in the Stortinge in connection with today's debate. In the four budget debates I have had during this major period in connection with the research budgets, the Minister of Industry has never been present either. Another State Council — the Minister for Fisheries — has been present in all these debates and has participated actively and positively. It says respect. But the Minister of Industry's absence is alarming. I refer to the Right proposal and the proposals on which the Right is included, which may not have been raised by other representatives in the past.

Unifying family life and a research career are demanding. I believe this may have a negative impact both on the recruitment of younger researchers in general and on the recruitment of women in particular. Postdoctor Helene Aarseth at the University of Oslo says this: ‘I can notice a rather strong pressure against a normalisation of the ‘free’ researcher. That is to say, a researcher who is free from care and family responsibilities." Philosophy professor Christel Fricke of the same university says: «European universities are undergoing a transformation. UiO is no exception. An academic career is not family friendly.» Fellow student May-Linda Magnussen at Agderforschung says this: ‘The requirement is to work around the clock and dedicate one’s life to research.’ In Sweden, there is also a broad consensus among researchers that priority should be given to the job ahead of all other responsibilities in life. In 2012, the University of Gothenburg carried out a report called «Jamstälter fakulter?» Here, women's and men's assumptions in academia were surveyed. The report’s interview study revealed that employees’ lives need to adapt to their professional life — not vice versa. The willingness of the employee to embrace this adaptation is decisive as to whether he or she will and can continue to research, say it. I will be surprised if the situation is particularly different in Norway than in Sweden. Young researchers are a group that is often in the middle of the establishment phase. Therefore, it will be very important to put in place arrangements to make it easier to combine a research career with a family life. In today’s setting, the Christian People’s Party is therefore promoting a proposal to evaluate a model that addresses this challenge. The Action Plan for the Swiss National Science Foundation 2013–2016 sets out a model called the ‘120 % model’ to meet this challenge. The aim of this model is to contribute to more flexible career planning for the target population and to prevent the delay or non-completion of research activities. This model allows the postdoctor to reduce working time, where a 60 pst position is the lowest limit. Positions with reduced working time may have up to 20 pst. financial support in addition to the initial rate of employment, which shall be used to employ so-called ‘supporters’ — technicians, laboratory assistants, scientific assistants, etc. – to conduct studies and discharge tasks. The additional costs will normally be up to a maximum of 20 pst. I think that a similar action in Norway could make it easier to keep researchers in the early childhood phase, and not least I think it could have a good impact on the female profile. Tomorrow it's the Jubilee of Voting Rights here in our country, and women and women's entrances have been important everywhere. That is why the Christian People's Party has also chosen to spend time in this debate raising this issue. Because we have a major problem in the academy. Academia is far too much of a human favorite. The requirements for successful academic careers are poorly consistent with the need to combine care and careers. Therefore, I am probably disappointed that the government parties do not have a more offensive attitude to this in the vote. In the Research Communication, the Government describes that they will engage in dialogue with the Kif Committee on Gender Balance in Research to find action. Kif has made many proposals for action, and the government now had the opportunity to make concrete proposals. This chance didn't use it. The Christian People's Party has received messages from young women in the academy who tell of lack of organisation in the institutions, and who are calling for clearer strategy and will to act. It is important to have good and clear plans for gender equality, setting targets and reporting results. At the same time, it is a challenge that institutions often have such good equality strategies, but fail in their implementation and follow-up. The diversity of scientific staff is important because it has an impact both on student role models and on who controls knowledge development. Recruitment and employment of scientific staff requires awareness of gender balance, and measures to counter existing structural inequalities must be available. A prerequisite for getting enough women into scientific positions is both that there are female students in all disciplines, and that students are encouraged to choose a further academic career. Measures such as gender scores may be considered where there are longer-term difficulties in recruiting an underrepresented gender. We see that women traditionally choose safe careers, and a reduction in the number of temporary jobs could make the academy a more real alternative for more women. Several have pointed out that women take greater responsibility for the educational activity of institutions. This has the consequence that they may not spend as much time as men on research activities. And when we know that it's research activity, and not education, that formally underlies the assessment of the professional competence of a person, it will contribute to the strengthening of gender imbalance. In this big message, we outline several ambitious actions to create more top-class research and education environments in the international community, to better facilitate boldness and innovation in the research system, to contribute to the development of business and the public sector, to stimulate stronger internationalisation in parts of the system – that there is a good link between national and international instruments – and to facilitate even better interaction on the development and use of knowledge. No 30 for 2008-2009, Climate for Research – and which is now creating uncertainty around the right – because we need to live up to what is the theme of the Communication, namely long lines. We shall contribute to meeting global challenges; we shall provide high-quality health and care services; we shall strengthen research-based welfare policies and the provision of welfare services; we shall promote a knowledge-based business across the country; and we shall contribute to business development in the areas of food, marine, maritime, travel, energy, environment, biotechnology, ICT, new materials and nanotechnologies. To achieve this, Norway shall have a well-functioning research system. We need high quality research, we need a high degree of internationalisation, and we need efficient use of research resources and results. In addition, the government will stress that the quality of education is very important for society’s ability to change and innovation. It is by making better use of our resources — labour with increased knowledge, competence and the use of technology — that we need to ensure that the key factors for conversion and value creation in the Norwegian economy are in place. The growth in allocations to industry through the Research Council from 2005 to 2011 has been 74pst. This is the most marked growth in the period, mainly due to the growth in the allocation of user-driven research. But we can improve this, because our quota is probably going to be about about. 16 B.C.E. until 2020. We're going to have a strategy to make the most of the quota money we give, both in terms of the areas in which we participate, how we can support Norwegian candidate processes, and how we can help equip Norwegian research environments to guide us in this direction. But we also need to ensure that we have a space for action on national priorities, both because there are many peculiarities associated with these EU programmes, and because there are some areas where Horizon 2020 will not give priority to our own needs. This year’s Research Communication is distinct from previous research notifications by placing a significant emphasis on the relationship between research and education. Research contributes to knowledge development. Quality of education contributes to innovation, and the new global academic sharing culture through the Internet has provided us with examples of global knowledge sharing, cited by world-renowned academics and universities. This may mean revolutionary opportunities for democratisation, application and dissemination of knowledge. It is essential that those working on research have good framework conditions for doing a good job. Therefore, many of the measures announced in the communication are precisely targeted at it. I'm very happy. I am very pleased that the proposal on entry positions has been received so well in many environments. It provides a possibility of greater predictability. We have announced that we will step up efforts against temporary employment. There is a very important part now in the governance dialogue with different institutions, where those institutions that have increased or still high levels of temporaryity can be required to create their own action plans to reduce the temporaryity. So this is very important. I am also very pleased that the Committee is fully unanimous in terms of open availability – open access – and the ambitions we have in this area. This is a good starting point for an offensive research policy for the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e1401	The Committee has done a good job in examining the Research Communication and has addressed a number of topics of major importance for research and value creation for the future. The Progress Party will point out that the Quality Reform for Higher Education has led to much more results-oriented research activities at universities and higher education institutions. The so-called teller edge system has been controversial and criticised for creating bureaucracy, but the results show strong growth in the number of publications, doctoral degrees, etc. The numerator edge system is not perfect. We know, among other things: that it may seem unnecessary bureaucratic. The drive party will maintain the system, but believes that the system needs to be improved, simplified and reinforced at the same time. Here we have to change to preserve. Among other things, commercialisation activities and cooperation between academia and business should be incorporated into the counter-border system. Furthermore, I would like to stress that the result-based system does not have to unilaterally focus on quantity, but to at least the same extent it must promote quality in education and research. The government has announced a comprehensive review of the funding of higher education and research. In this respect, the Progress Party will point out that the universities of Stavanger, Agder and Nordland have changed their status from regional colleges to universities. As state universities, these have had different and worse framework conditions than established universities and scientific colleges, especially in the area of research funding. The new universities have a special opportunity to strengthen research in the professional professions, but today’s funding provides an undesirable discrimination against their research efforts. We believe that this should be corrected. The Progress Party hopes that an overall review will also include a review of funding for private universities. A reasonable framework for grants must be found in consultation with the institutions, both with regard to the establishment of new places of study and grants to the infrastructure. Several private colleges have problems maintaining a good infrastructure for the students, and it is necessary to make changes in funding to allow them to do so without transferring the costs to the students through increased tuition fees. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e1538	It's fundamental for research to build on accumulated knowledge. This means that it is necessary for the researcher to have access to national and international knowledge status and sources of knowledge — and it is demanding. The amount of research publications has increased, and as knowledge has become an increasingly important driving force in the economy, the costs of access to research sources have also increased. It is not that new technology has reduced the cost of the technical and research libraries or research institutions. On the contrary, expenditure has gone right in the past. In most cases, researchers have an interest in sharing their results with others — it is the publication that gives research status. It is also that you have an interest in sharing from the standpoint: if I get access to your research, you get access to mine. We all depend on that. That's what's at the root of open publishing. I am very pleased that the government is so clearly saying in the message that it basically believes that all the research that is fully or partly publicly funded should be open to the public, and that this receives unanimous support from the committee. It is important to stress that open publication will largely be in addition to established scientific journals. Neither does open access mean that it will be free of charge for the institutions building up their scientific archives. For the time being, the price per publication in open archives is still above the price per publication in subscription databases. The difference is that in the first case the costs are charged to the institution contributing to archiving, while in the second case the institution or researcher seeking access is charged. Both of these ways of publishing will live next to each other for a while. Open publication is to establish a knowledge-based public opinion alongside the commercial market. It is necessary for commercial publication to have a competitor or counterpower to prevent monopolisation. The strict self-archiving requirements imposed by the authorities in open archives will help commercial publishers to accept that publication takes place in parallel. Building open archives is therefore an important task for institutions. It gets more expensive, but it gives access to smaller institutions. In parallel with that, we still have to have access to the commercial publications. Then it's important that the job that Cristin does – and then I don't think of the minister, but of the Cristin database system – to get access and licensing to databases continues. Open publishing is therefore not a way to save money, but a way to democratize access to research results.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e1647	There is an enormous need for engineers in industry, maritime nutrition and oil and gas. Developments in these industries are such that there will be an ever-increasing need for engineers in the coming period. Large investment from different industries will require increased engineering skills. Engineering needs in Norway will be constantly high and growing. The huge shortage of engineers leads in particular to two things: the wages for engineers are being pushed up, and an increasing proportion of engineers are being picked up from abroad. High wages are nice for engineers, but challenging for business. The Norwegian salary level in the competitive industry is currently 70 pst. higher than competitors in other countries. It goes without saying that it's almost impossible to compete with such assumptions. In addition, when we see a real decline in grants for business-related research, we can risk not being able to compete for innovation and innovation. So we're going to praise ourselves. At the same time, increasing labour-gathering from abroad will not only drain other countries for badly needed skilled labour, but also increase the risk that the skills we currently possess will one day move out of the country again. There are alarming numbers who are jumping off engineering training. As many as 30 pst.–40 pst. of those starting from the engineering study, do not complete the education. And most of those who jump off are struggling with mathematics. NOCUT has shown that several of the low-searched colleges took students with the grade two in mathematics from high school. When we know that the introduction of four or better grade requirements in mathematics for admission to the NTNU Civil Engineering Study has produced very good results and reduced non-response, there is good reason to believe that a character requirement for engineering training will have the same effect. Despite the government's real science strategy, the proportion of students who choose the most difficult mathematics in the third crash goes down. From over 7 000 students with the most advanced mathematics in 2008, the number today is below 6 000 students. So it's a fall in absolute numbers over a period of time when the juveniles are increasing. Unfortunately, today it is not a good enough offer for those who are very good in mathematics. With advanced mathematics as a new subject of choice, the students who choose it will be able to go up the level of ordinary mathematics as well. Engineering training is likely to be the least economically rational training for educational institutions. It is the education that has the lowest income compared to expenditure. The cost of the engineering study with the need for equipment, laboratories and personal guidance is so costly that it belongs to cost category D. In addition, it is essential that the capacity of civil engineering studies be expanded as far as possible without any particular decrease in the quality of the students being admitted. There will probably be room to accommodate another 500 more students per year, without the quality of the candidates falling dramatically. However, today’s infrastructure in the study places is not possible. The entry for scientific equipment needs to be expanded and targeted efforts must be made to expand laboratory capacity in study sites offering engineering studies, such as NTNU and other study facilities in regions with high growth and an increasing need for engineers. In addition, facilitating transitions between the engineering study and the civil engineering study will help to increase the number of civil engineering students.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e1834	The Family and Culture Committee has discussed Prop. 120 L for 2012–2013 on amendments to the Kindergarten Act, police record, etc. In the bill, the government proposes changes to the regulations of the Children’s Home Office Act on Police Certificate and Reporting of Child Home Service Data. As the rapporteur and as part of the majority, I will present the comments and position of the Christian People's Party and the government parties in the vote. I'm counting on the minority to take the floor to brief on their views. The Christian People's Party and the government parties support the legislative proposals as they exist in the proposition. The amendments mean that the provisions of the Kinderhagen Act on Police Certificate are consistent with the overall framework of the Police Register Act, that the two laws are harmonised with regard to the extent of criminal penalties to be imposed by police attestations, that there is an absolute requirement for the submission of police attestations to all those employed permanently or temporarily in the Kinderhagen, and that kindergarten owners and municipalities are granted legal access to require police certification also by persons without recruitment who have the opportunity to be in direct contact with the young. Furthermore, the amendments to the law clearly extend the infringements of the criminal law to be noted on police records issued under the Children's Hospital Act, the fact that pending cases relating to sexual abuse against both minors and adults are noted on the police record, and the possible need for new or up-to-date information by children's homeowners is made apparent by the wording of the Children's Home Office Act, which refers to the Police Register Act, Section 43. The Government agrees that the Children’s Hospital Act does not lay down specific rules on police attestations for those who have lived in other countries, in addition to those already provided for by the Police Register Act. This means that no legal requirement for residence is introduced for persons from outside the EEA territory. The Committee also supports the Government's proposal for amendments to Section 7 of the Kindergarten Act on Service Data Reporting. When making the kind of changes we are making today, it is important to consider what offences should be included in a police record in the context of care-related work. There are many crimes in a person's history that can be of a nature that do not necessarily make him less fit to work with children in kindergarten. It may be related to a growing-up environment. This may be due to the fact that young people have belonged to a particular environment. It may also be linked to a period of drug dependence, etc. I am pleased that we are now adopting a law that strengthens the protection of young people, that strengthens the safety of kindergartens, and that strengthens the control of those employed to care for young people in kindergarten. Nevertheless, it is important to make reasonable adjustments in order to ensure that the legislation is sensible. In this context, it is worth mentioning that the Stortinget will also discuss child protection in the next case on today's map, where an important theme is post-preservation and bringing people back to society after a troubled childhood, and where it is about giving young people the opportunity to get up after a difficult start in life. Overall, the majority of the committee believes that the government has found a good balance where the sum is that we strengthen the protection of young people and that we extend the requirements for what should be documented per police certificate to become a child care worker. The majority therefore recommend that the legislative changes in the proposal be adopted and are well-satisfied with the outcome.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e1866	Parents who give kindergarten responsibility for their children’s large parts of the day must be sure that the child is surrounded by competent, stable and safe adults. Both the Children’s Ombudsman, the Education Directorate, the Barnhage Law Committee, the National Association of Private Barnhage, the Education Association and the Parental Committee of Kindergartens have highlighted the need to ensure the safety of children in kindergarten. In kindergarten, we need employees who can set limits both for themselves and for others. It is then important that the employer is provided with sufficient information about applicants when he/she is employed, so that the employer can carefully assess whether he/she will employ people who have a past in which they have committed acts that are considered incompatible with working in kindergarten. If a person has been imprisoned for up to four years for harming others or has broken the drug law and abused drugs, the Right believes that the employer must have the opportunity to gain knowledge of this before hiring someone in the kindergarten. The right would therefore suggest that the police certificate also provide the employer with information on matters such as body damage, violation of the drug law rule on drugs and prescription management. Persons convicted of gross violence, trafficking in human beings or violence in close relationships should be excluded from work in kindergarten, and the Right proposes that this be incorporated into the law. Until now, no governor has been able to ask for new police statements. The new law on police records opens up this, but only if the conditions for police certificates are still present. This means, therefore, cases where the employer has access to information which gives reason to believe that new information is available of importance. This means that the employer must be aware that the employee has committed new offences in order to ask for a new certificate. In the Right, we believe that there is reason to ask questions about whether this meets the children’s need for safety and security in kindergarten, and there are many key actors who believe that there should be an opportunity to obtain police attestations in the sector. The right-hand side supports this. The safety of children must come first, and we therefore propose that there should be a general possibility of a new background check for information other than that provided on the basis of which the original police certificate was issued. The right is pleased that provisions on child care certificates have been improved in the context of updating the Police Register Act, but we believe that the proposal is not sufficient, that it should be made clearer and more stringent. With this, I take up the proposals from the Right and the Advance Party.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e1879	The representative Linda C. Hofstad Helleland has addressed the proposals she referred to.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e1882	The main purpose of the amendment of the police record rules is to ensure that children in kindergarten are protected from being placed in a trusting or dependent relationship with persons who may cause them harm and/or adversely affect them. It is also important to have clear rules on police certificates in order to increase the trust of parents and others in the care of children in kindergartens. The development of recent years, with an increasing number of children in kindergarten, requires that there is no doubt that there are only suitable people who associate with our children in kindergarten everyday life. This suggests that the rules on background control, inter alia: police certificates are amended in such a way that persons who have committed an offence against minors and persons who have a harmful influence on children are excluded from working in kindergartens. In the context of the introduction of the new Police Register Act, the Ministry of Justice carried out thorough assessments of the offences which should be recorded on the police record of persons to be employed in work involving contact with children and minors. In the work on the legislative proposal, the Ministry of Knowledge has made a similar assessment with a view to legislating the offences to be noted on police statements to be made before employment in kindergarten. The legislative proposal provides for important harmonisation with the overall framework of the Police Register Act, which the Stortinget has adopted, to enter into force and replace the criminal record law of 1971. Today, the requirement is that police attestations based on the Children’s Hospital Act show whether they are intended, accused or convicted of sexual abuse against children. I believe it is important to change the legal provision so that the employer has a broader basis for assessing the suitability of employment. The proposal under consideration today represents a substantial extension of the number of infringements of the criminal law to be noted on the police record for those seeking employment in kindergarten. Following the proposal, all types of sexual offences shall be noted, regardless of whether the offender is a child or an adult. Any violation of criminal law rules on drug, poaching and violence shall be identified. In addition, it shall be stated not only whether the person concerned is intentional, accused or convicted, but also whether the person has adopted a proposal in the light of such infringements. The proposal provides a much better basis for all kindergarten owners to assess whether a jobseeker is unsuitable for working in kindergarten. There is reason to point out that the obligation to provide police clearance following the proposed action does not apply only to those seeking permanent or temporary employment. In order to protect the children, I have considered it necessary that the obligation should also cover all persons who are regularly in kindergarten, for example. janitor and cleaning staff and that persons with significant influence on the operation of kindergartens should also be included, e.g. the owner or the Executive Director. Some offenders are considered particularly relevant for the purpose of protecting children and young people. This applies in particular to offences related to sexual offences against minors. For this reason, a professional ban in kindergarten is proposed for persons who have committed such acts. The potential for damage is particularly high in this type of crime. It is therefore necessary to give decisive and particularly heavy weight to reasons for protection. Persons who have committed this type of crime should therefore not even be able to be assessed for employment in kindergarten on the basis of the proposal. It was for the hearing bodies to extend the professional ban to several of the other offences to be noted on the police record after the proposal. With this, I would like to stress that the question has been carefully assessed in the legislative proposal. The need for protection and the importance of life that speaks of including as much information as possible on the police record is carefully weighed against privacy considerations, resource considerations and resiliency considerations — and it is inconvenient to include unnecessary information. This is an important assessment, and I would like to stress that the proposal for a ban on work which has been promoted is in substance the same as the Church, Education and Research Committee and the Judicial Committee have given their support through the adoption of the Training Act, the Private School Act and the Police Register Act. Finally, I would like to stress that we must not believe that rules and police statements are in themselves adequate protection for children in kindergarten. The usefulness of these rules must not be overestimated. In the future, it is also essential for children’s safety that the nursery care centre, through its employer’s responsibility, follow up all recruitment processes with thorough interviews and reference acquisitions. It is only a rare time that there are people who have previously been targeted, accused or convicted of serious crimes, who report as applicants for kindergartens – and therefore have a leaflet that makes you know that you are facing a person who can threaten children. In the vast majority of cases, offenders are not, or have been, punished or targeted in such cases. Therefore, be aware!

Through thorough investigation and input from those who are most competent in the field, including the children's seas, we have come up with a number of proposals for change, a little on organisation, but also on legal work and what we are going to do in the future, in particular to follow up what is our intention, that we should come in quickly and give early help to children who need it, and also give better help to families, so that they can function, those who have the potential to care for their own children. That's also a goal for us. Why do you have to listen to the kids? Well, because the children in child protection are the main characters, because it's about respect, it's about security for them, it's about quality, it's about stability, it's about success security of what we're doing, it's about self-esteem, self-esteem for children, insight into their own situation, acceptance for decisions, better cooperation, which in turn leads to better stability and better quality. At all: there are no good reasons not to listen to children. We have to get a lot better at it. The document we are discussing today bears the mark that we have listened to the children. What are the kids want? Among other things, they want a trust person. They want better supervision. They want other concepts of child protection. They will be involved, which we now legalize. They want us to get in faster with good help. And yes, they want better stability. What's important in order to achieve better stability, in addition to helping children, is that we get in faster. The new principles also come into play, including: the new principle of quality of connection and relationship. If we are unable to provide more rapid assistance to children, so that they are not so damaged when we first choose to place them, after having tried for many years – often in the certainty that what we put in action, unfortunately not building up during the change – it is also difficult to offer stability to many of those children. Then they will be quite damaged when they are first placed in a home, which in turn will affect their stay at the foster home or at the institution, for that matter. Early delivery of assistance to these children can also contribute to greater security and stability. We are also committed to better cooperation – across and across – and overall efforts and cooperation between different agencies, which are important in order for children to have a better future. It will be absolutely vital for us to work along those lines also in the years to come.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e2634	This evening, the Grand Council adopts a completely new law that will provide full protection for homosexuals, lesbians, biphiles and trans-persons in all areas of society. Today this applies only to working life and housing. This is in many ways a new brand day, not just for the red-green government, but for all gays, lesbians, biphiles and trans-persons, by putting in place a full protection against discrimination. In the spring of 2008, we adopted common marriage law, so today we are adopting full protection against discrimination. The law will provide protection against discrimination on the grounds of sexual orientation in all areas of society, both public and private, health and care services, outpatients, organisations, etc. We know that discrimination and harassment of homosexuals, lesbians and biphilis are still a major problem in many areas of society. Unfortunately, it is documented in several studies that homosexuals, including in Norway in 2013, are exposed to violence and threats in public space. It's not just happening in Eastern Europe. The law also proposes that gender identity and gender expression should become a separate basis for discrimination. An explicit protection of discrimination will not only make today’s legal text clearer and more informative, but will also give a signal of equivalency for trans-persons. The working party considers this to be important, not least in order to counteract negative and prejudiced attitudes towards trans-persons. These are particularly vulnerable and exposed to violence, harassment and discrimination that reduces the quality of life and their ability to participate in society. The Committee has experienced strong reactions from Harry Benjamin Resource Centre to the use of the concept of transpersons. Their patient group has undergone gender-confirming treatment, and they do not want to go under the collective term trans-persons. The background for this is that many in their group don't define themselves as transperson. We have great respect for these points of view, but at the same time I would like to stress that some of the intention to include gender identity and gender expression in the law is to help change society's attitudes and counteract negative and prejudiced attitudes. The term of office of the Discrimination Law Committee was the exercise of a common law. The government parties believe that it is essential not that the protection of discrimination be incorporated into a law but that the protection of discrimination is effectively strengthened. It does so in this proposal. Government parties believe that a separate law on gender equality provides the best framework for work on gender equality. Similarly, we believe that our own laws will provide the best framework for gender equality in terms of ethnicity, religion and disability. In a common discriminatory law, the individual grounds for discrimination could not be highlighted in the same way as in the specific laws. The Equality Act applies to both women and men, but when the law was passed, the legislator acknowledged that women in many areas of society are more discriminated against than men. This is made visible, inter alia: in the provision of the law’s purpose that the law ‘aims in particular at improving the position of women’. It is still the case that in many cases women are more discriminated against than men. This shows, for example: statistics on matters of equality and discrimination by the Ombudsman. The government's proposal on pregnancy protection has met with criticism. The Government proposes that a clarification be included in the prohibition of discrimination which states that discrimination on grounds of pregnancy and maternity leave or adoption is to be regarded as sex discrimination. I would like to stress strongly that the legislative changes that this entails in the current equality law are continuing with the existing law on the protection of discrimination in respect of pregnancy, childbirth and parental leave. The text of the legislation is brought more into line with current practice, which means that both mother and father throughout the parental leave have a particularly strong protection and, in many cases, an absolute protection. A number of actors, including: The Equality and Discrimination Ombudsman has been concerned that this adjustment in practice will weaken the protection of pregnant women. Nor is the intention of the legislator the opposite. Legitimate practices will ensure continued strong protection of pregnancy. Through the changes in the law, we give a strong protection to the entire period of parental leave, and not as today, only the weeks that are earmarked for mother and father. This is a brand day. Tomorrow, the Duma in Russia will adopt restrictions on the rights of homosexuals, in stark contrast to what we are adopting today in the Norwegian Grand Duchy, where we are now banning all forms of discrimination against homosexuals, lesbians, biphiles and trans-persons.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e2685	The aims of the Discrimination Act are equivalency, equal opportunities and rights, equal availability and organisation where it is needed — in short, equality in all that that term may mean. Good and clear legislation is, of course, primarily in terms of what the law is supposed to protect, but not least as a signal of what our society represents. The fact that we have clear legislation tells us about a society based on values that are about the fact that discrimination in any context and prejudice – as it pleases us – are ghosts that can lie hidden for a long time and that can suddenly become apparent when given situations occur. Not least these attitudes relate to ethnicity or religion. It is of great and self-esteem that a comprehensive political environment in Norway has such a clear view of this. Building a tolerant society is not done with the laws alone, but is also about the debates that take place in public space – the conversations in the workplace, in the family, in school, in short, all communities that make up our society. The proposed legislative changes are, as the Right sees it, essentially moving in the right direction. The right, therefore, supports the intentions and the clean-up that have been done, even if we had initially wanted a different main action on this legislation. Of course, we support the progress that is now being made in terms of discrimination based on orientation, and we are very pleased that this is now coming into place. But we also notice that the organisations involved, just as they would have seen a common general, general law. No discrimination shall be tolerated with the eyes of the Right. One discrimination basis is not more important than the other. The fact that some grounds, such as discrimination on grounds of sex, religion or ethnicity, are more widespread and more visible, should not mean that other grounds for discrimination are worse or should have a lower level of protection – not to say no protection, which is unfortunately the case in some contexts. What is a basis for discrimination could vary and, as a legislator, it is difficult to predict all the contexts in which this protection is really necessary. We need general, general legislation to protect against discrimination. From this point of view, the Right would have wanted a different approach to the statutory audit, and with the support of the Discrimination Committee's investigation and many of the hearing bodies that applied there, the Right believes it would be right to draw up a single law which would also provide space with a general ban on discrimination. In this context, we note that the government itself in the proposition points out that one common law would provide legal technical improvements, better protection against complex discrimination and – not least – a clearer signal effect. The argument against a collective law is that it would give vague signals about what is to be perceived, for example. to the employers of the country. The position of the right is clear: what is covered must be discrimination in general. I think those who discriminate know when it happens. Our trust in the employers is as well-known. We don't think any work is served by discrimination of any kind. Nor do we believe that there will be major problems if the courts have to deal with an open definition and, through case-law, give the content of what it should be. This has been a discussion for many years, and in the past, the Right has also proposed a law with a universal approach. As in the past, we are also promoting proposals in this crossroads. We see developments going our way, but we're a little surprised that the government doesn't follow up the same way. As politicians, we often meet press groups who want to pay particular attention to their issues and angles. Equality is a fundamental value and shall apply to all, irrespective of gender, sexual orientation, ethnicity, level of function, etc. We have a good protection against discrimination in Norway. Law on gender equality – or the Gender Equality Act – was passed 35 years ago, and since then the Discrimination Act has been extended and improved. Legal protection against discrimination currently includes not only gender but also ethnicity and disability. I am delighted that we are now dealing with the legislative proposal that provides gay, lesbian, biphile and transpersonnel – LHBT people – protection against discrimination in all areas of society, and not as today that it concerns only employment and housing. In addition to prohibiting discrimination, the legislative proposal also provides for public authorities, social organisations, educational institutions and employers to have an obligation to work actively to promote equality and to prevent harassment and discrimination – which is very important. If the discrimination and harassment to which LHBT persons are subjected are to be dealt with, it does not make it clear that discrimination is prohibited. Schools, sports teams and employers must then take responsibility for combating stereotypes and prejudices and prevent harassment. The aim of the law is to promote equality. Norwegian law ensures formal equality in many areas, but effective enforcement and enforcement is needed. It is therefore important to strengthen legal rights, including attitudes, in order to ensure a non-discriminatory society. There has been a clear expectation of a more comprehensive and reinforced protection of discrimination in Norwegian legislation. Many have thought that we should have a common law covering all forms of discrimination and all areas of society. When it is not proposed, it is because it is feared that the protection of women from discrimination could be weakened by the repeal of the Equality Act. SV believes that the objective of a more comprehensive and enhanced protection of discrimination has been achieved through the proposal, as the laws have been harmonised and more coherent. It is important that the protection of women against discrimination remains strong. Most people agree that women in many areas are more discriminated against than men. Good lawmaking is by no means enough in itself, and attitudes and organisation are also necessary. Quotation has been, and is, one known measure in this way – something of which the Progress Party is fundamentally opposed. The most common form of quotaing takes place by favouring the underrepresented when candidates are equally qualified — and this has definitely contributed to correcting some societal imbalances. Being a principal opponent of any kind of quota builds up under the fact that sex, ethnicity, etc. in itself in many, it is considered a qualification. I can mention here measures that have been used to increase the recruitment of people with a migrant background to work. Evidence of positive results has been obtained by calling at least one person with a migrant background for interview provided that the applicant is qualified for the job. The biggest problem is that they are not called for an interview. When they get the opportunity to show their qualifications through an interview, many of them get a job. It's a positive form of quota. There are alarming people who experience sexual harassment – something we have a legal ban on in the Equality Act. Employers and the management of educational institutions and organisations are required to prevent sexual harassment. Although I had primarily seen that the Equality and Discrimination Ombudsman was to enforce the prohibition of sexual harassment in line with the other prohibitions in the Equality Act, I am pleased that the government is now asking to investigate the establishment of a guidance service for persons who are subjected to sexual harassment. The important thing is that they are met by an auxiliaries with information about rights and opportunities. The law to ensure equality and non-discrimination is, as has been said, good and is now even better with the protection of discrimination for LHBT individuals. But it is a problem that, in most cases, discrimination is completely free of charge. The protection of discrimination is enforced by the Equality and Discrimination Ombudsman and the Equality and Discrimination Department, but neither the Ombudsman nor the Ombudsman has the possibility of giving up even if it is established that discrimination has occurred. If you are going to get an uproar, you have to take the case to the court. In practice, those who can and can afford to do so on their own account are reserved. One step along the way is to ask the government to investigate the role of enforcement and lowthreshold agencies and alternative ways of establishing a lowthreshold offer for uplifting in discrimination cases. The adoption of a law that prohibits discrimination against homosexuals and transpersons is a milestone in ensuring human rights for all – and at least we have reason to be satisfied with that.
