ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e2909	The Judicial Committee has dealt with Proposition 101 L for 2012–2013 on the Law on Arbitration. In the recommendation, the Committee notes that it is important for a country to have a good regulatory framework for the right to land change, which will give landowners a systematic regulatory approach. It could therefore be anti-conflict and solution-oriented and would reduce the lengthy and cost-effective processes of such cases before the General Courts. The 1979 Land Change Act has needed a significant revision, and the government's proposal for a new law will be a good tool for the right to land change in a modern age. After a change in legislation a few years ago, land change can also be carried out in cities and urban areas and not only in traditional agricultural areas. It is also emphasised in the new law that many self-proclaiming parties are involved in land change issues, so that a legal and process system that is easily accessible to them is important. Furthermore, the Committee notes in its recommendation that the basic principles of land change be passed on from the previous law, that the main purpose is to provide land change with a total net benefit to landowners, and that the process should not result in costs or disadvantages exceeding the benefits of any property or right of use. The Committee has taken note of the need to update the Law on Land Change in relation to other major legislative audits, including the Law on Disputes, the Law on Planning and Building and the Law on Law on Law on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on Laws on In this case, it is proposed that the Land Change Courts should be merged with the Lawyers' Rights and that the Lawyers' Rights should be strengthened with the professional competence of the land change. This will add to the rights of the team members the competences that can also be used in addition to the traditional soil change cases. The Committee is behind this. Furthermore, in dealing with appeals against a decision in the right to change the law of the land, the law of the law of the law of the land shall be set up differently, as far as the anchors or anchors are concerned. In some cases, there shall be a soil change layer. In other cases, it is up to the first-layer to decide that land change competence shall be withdrawn. The First Lawyer may also, in some cases, decide that both land change judges and a team judge shall be together in court. In some cases, co-judges may be used in court, while in other contexts such participation has not been envisaged. A minority – members of the Committee from the Progress Party and the Right – took note of the fact that several parent organisations in the consultation round have shown considerable scepticism for the proposal to empower the land change rights to form a common ownership. These members largely share this scepticism. Same as agricultural real estate ownership is not advised in other contexts. Therefore, these members will request that this possibility be exercised to a limited extent. At the hearing of the Committee, a number of views were expressed on the fact that the government has proposed a significant increase in the procedural fee for the appeal of land change cases in the Law of Lawyers. Today, the parties pay five times the legal fee, while the bill introduces a fee of 24 times the legal fee without providing sufficient justification, in the understanding of the minority. Several hearing bodies, at the hearing of the Committee, stated that this could limit the appeals of some parties due to the costs involved. During the Committee's proceedings, the Committee sent a written question to the Government on this subject and received a relatively long response. In this context, there was also a call from the Court of Justice with a proposal for a legal paragraph which deviated somewhat from the government’s proposal. This is why the minority has chosen to follow the draft legislative paragraph of the Court of Justice in this area. Furthermore, the Committee points to the long-term recruitment problems in the area of the right to change and asks for a significant focus to be placed on this. I therefore refer to the proposal that arises from the vote.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e3004	I'm happy to hear such a cohesive big thing, and that a cohesive big thing says that this is an important law and a law with great social significance. The main purpose of the revision of the law on land change is to create a more modern law for the operation of the law on land change. The legislative proposal involves a better legal structure and a clearer distinction between instruments and procedural rules. The legislative proposal also provides for a better use of the special expertise of the law on land change. Legal certainty is strengthened by a simpler and clearer appeal mechanism. The Land Change Act establishes the land change rights and authorizes their work. The main activity of the right to land change is to improve difficult and non-profitable operating conditions, to clarify boundaries between properties and to obtain unclear rights. The right to land change also provides some practical follow-up in the field of land-handling and land-handling. The general courts may, in the same way as the right to change the land, settle the legal situation relating to real estate. However, they may not establish new and more appropriate property or forms of cooperation between owners and rightholders, as may be the case with the right to land change. The new law will mainly pass on the means by which the rights of land change can be exercised today. Soil rights shall continue to be able to switch to parcels, establish, move and abolish rights, form cooperation teams and lay down articles of association, etc. The legal instruments will facilitate more appropriate ownership and use conditions both in agricultural areas and in more urban areas. The law facilitates the development of construction sites by distributing planned value-added between landowners. Moreover, under the new law, disputes between the parties will not be necessary in order to raise a case. It is enough that the parties believe that their properties are difficult to exploit in a decent way, or that they believe the boundaries are unclear and will have help to clarify them. The legislative proposal will build on the fact that the right to change the land will try to obtain amicable solutions. In the case of parties who are prosecuting themselves in court, the obligation to provide guidance is important. The legislative proposal leads to a better structure of the rules on discretion. The law implies a more comprehensible use of the concept of discretion. The law also creates greater cleanliness because there are fewer actors in matters of judgement. The special expertise of the law on land change is better exploited and the rules on treatment are simplified. The main reform is the changes to the appeal mechanism. Today's system of two appeals, the law of the team and the law of the land change, poses a risk of misappropriation, and thus risks legal loss because the anchor cannot be taken during treatment. After the bill, there will be only one appeal - the law of the law. The competences of the Earthquake Supreme Court are transferred to the Law of Lawyers through the introduction of a new jurisdiction — the Landchange Layer Judge. The geothermallayer shall be involved in the treatment of all appeals for which land change competence is necessary. Strengthening the law of the team with shunting judges may also have a positive effect on other neighbouring types of cases. The law allows the land changer to participate in other property matters if there is capacity to do so. The rules on the costs of the case and the fee are mainly passed on. I have noticed that the Committee has differed in its views on the proposal for amendments to the appeal fee. I take note that the majority of the committee concludes on the government's proposals. The appeal fee to the Lawyers' Court will be the same with the amendments to the law in all the forms of decision that the right to change the land may take. Recruitment to land change judgment positions is a challenge. There are not many qualified applicants for the posts advertised. Some people have already done something to improve this, for example: through improved pay conditions and the abolition of the educational monopoly for land change judges who have had the Agricultural Higher Education College, now the University of Environment and Life Sciences. Several educational institutions will be in the early stages of relevant training, and I think this will improve recruitment in the short term. Practitioners of the Sami reindeer may now be party to proceedings for the right to land change in relation to relations between the reindeer and other rightholders. The State's obligation to consult has been fulfilled in drawing up the bill. The proposals relating to Sámi interests have been accepted by Sametinget and the Norwegian Rein driftssamers Landsförbund. Finally, I would like to thank the Committee for dealing with this proposition in a proper and proper way. It's been a lot of work. It was a massive proposition that the committee got, and I'm impressed by how the case rapporteur presented the matter in a neat and straightforward way.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e3325	The party of progress looks with concern at today's development of increasingly aggressive begging. It has gradually become clear through media redundancies and police statements that begging also brings with it other, more serious, crimes, such as burglaries and brutality. When the Barriers Act was repealed in 2005, it was on the grounds that the number of beggars had been significantly reduced over a period of time. Furthermore, it was justified that beggaring should have a social policy focus more than a criminal policy. At the time, begging was something that largely concerned Norwegian citizens, which was also the subject of the rapporteur's main contribution. The opening of borders to Eastern Europe in 2005 has led to a greater number of current beggars travelling from other countries. Rumens form a major part of these. Through various media surveys, it has been seen that the Romanians organize themselves in groups, where individuals are placed in different places in the city and beg there. This form of organised begging often constitutes a cover for other, more serious forms of crime. Intrusions and gross thefts are not an unusual consequence of the begging problem. Less serious conditions, such as nasking and pocket thefts, also follow in the wake of the incoming beggar environment. Media surveys have recently shown how the number of handkerchiefs has exploded in both cities in general and in Oslo in particular. It is necessary to make better use of the scope of the Schengen Agreement than is currently the case. Increased attention to border controls with frequent random checks also within Schengen could help to prevent crime in Norwegian cities. I would also like to point out that several disturbing examples of trafficking in human beings have been seen in the Romanian beggar environment. A case dealt with in the Bergen district court some time ago included both the trade in and sexual abuse of children, and the children were forced to both theft and the sale of false gold jewelry. Unfortunately, the fact is that the Norwegian level of punishment and prison services – as we discussed in the previous case – do not appear preventative on people from countries with significantly higher levels of punishment and lower prison standards. Trafficking in human beings is such a serious crime that action is urgently needed. Increased police attention to trafficking in human beings combined with an immediate increase in the penalties for this type of crime will be appropriate and preventative measures. In an attempt to control the begging problem, a number of municipalities have today introduced registration obligations for beggars. This allows the police to expel beggars who are not allowed to beg, and is thus a small step in the right direction. However, this solution is not sufficient and will red tape and make police work more difficult compared to a general ban. There is also little reason to believe that the major challenges associated with today’s begging will disappear even if the beggars are ordered to have a municipal beggar’s permit. The Municipality of Oslo has repeatedly tried to go further and wants to implement a local total ban on begging. So this has not been possible under today's legislation. The willingness to solve problems in municipalities is generally high. It would therefore be wise to draw up arrangements allowing the municipalities themselves to introduce measures to combat begging. A proposal for a solution here could be municipal fines against illegal begging. This type of mechanism could relieve the police while strengthening local self-government. The Oslo police have clearly opposed today's regime and want a ban. Sources in the Oslo Police have stated that the situation is difficult to manage without a clear warrant for the expulsion of aggressive beggars. The establishment has thus said that one wants such a law - abiding place. It is therefore appropriate to be very careful to ignore clear calls from the department dealing with this problem every day. So unfortunately, the government chooses to turn the deaf ear to. The scale of the beggar problem has now become so wide that action is urgently needed. Trafficking in human beings, child exploitation, handkerchiefs and burglaries are criminal offences resulting from the cold water of beggars. An action plan on crime related to the beggar environment is necessary in order to control the situation, and with the current knowledge, there is strong concern that such an action plan – as proposed by the Progress Party in its proposal for a representative – also includes a general ban on begging. I am addressing the suggestions the Progress Party is promoting in the vote.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e3347	About 2 billion people live in poverty in our world. Over 1 billion people live in extreme poverty, they live for less than 1 dollar a day. If you think that the option is to go to school, then it's so far from what we know about the space population – especially in these Eastern European countries – that I'm getting a little scared. This is about countries where small-scale fascist – yes, semi-fascist – parties achieve a high degree of disintegration in elections, precisely because of their hatred of this group. There are people who are being killed because of their ethnicity. Yes, Europe has seen it before. Yes, the Norwegian Parliament should be ashamed of what was done at the end of the 1930s, when the Norwegian Roma were sent back – right in the concentration camps – by decision in this Chamber. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e3446	Tetting is undesirable, but we see with all manner of clarity that begging jumps out of social distress. The social and human challenges of this social distress are the country of origin of the individual, and poverty reduction is the primary responsibility of the countries of origin. Norway contributes 300 million to this work through the EEA funds. kr, but the government also looks at the possibility of increasing the allocation towards space and marginalised groups by channelling unused funds to these groups. Although begging is undesirable, I believe a total ban will be the wrong way to go, both because a ban will not remedy the poverty itself, with which we, as a society, have to endure to face, and because I believe a total ban will require police enforcement that cannot be defended as a resource, set up against other police tasks. However, the regulatory challenges posed by beggaring in some places must be taken seriously. What we are adopting today in Stortingen is going to help solve the regulatory challenges. Experience — not least from Oslo — shows that the number of beggars has increased and that beggaring some places is carried out in a way that creates public uncertainty. Information from the police also tells us that there are challenges related to crime among beggars. Typing is carried out in most municipalities to an extent and in a way that does not pose major regulatory challenges. However, there is a need to be able to take regulatory action in the municipalities where regulatory challenges arise, and that is the background to the proposal in the proposal. The proposal in the bill provides municipalities with a set of instruments. The police regulations may lay down conditions for the collection of funds. These conditions must be justified by their orderly nature. A municipality would be able to state prohibitions in certain geographical areas or timings or to leave such regulation entirely to the police. It is natural that there is dialogue between the municipality and the police on any areas to be affected. In areas where many people travel, where there is unrest, or where it is clearly distressing or disturbing the public, restrictions could be imposed. It's on the verge of, for example, traffic nodes and public transport are among the areas to which specific challenges may be attached. Municipalities may also choose to impose a reporting obligation on the police which would keep a register of those who have notified that they will collect money in a public place. This is an instrument that has been shown to have a damping effect on beggaring. It could also facilitate a better fight against crime in the beggar environment. In those municipalities where there is a disorder of the order of begging, the challenges must be met with thoughtful strategies, where police, municipalities and other public and private actors must cooperate closely. Resources need to be deployed in several areas, and legislative changes are intended to be useful tools for such a holistic approach. Because there are large local differences in the orderly challenges of beggaring, it is also natural for regulatory measures to be assessed locally by the municipalities. In the revised national budget, the Government has proposed to establish a grant scheme of 10 million. kr, where municipalities and organisations may seek funds for humanitarian action during the summer. The grant scheme has now been announced, and this is an important part of the overall government's wish to intervene in this area. Subsidies may be granted, for example: the establishment of shower and toilet facilities, and in some places it may also be relevant with the assistance of advisers with relevant language and cultural skills to inform about the regulatory needs of beggars. The proposers behind the representative proposal 96 S for 2012–2013 call for an action plan against beggar-related crime. In this context, I would like to point out that, while we presented our bill, the government also presented other measures that are suitable for improving the orderly situation. This will include, inter alia: become a lower threshold to expel foreigners who commit minor thefts.

During this ageing period, the Stortinget has discussed the victims’ compensation scheme several times, most recently twice in 2012. Today we are to decide on a proposal from individual members of the Progress Party. The proposal for a representative is based on a request that the new limit of 60 G be retroactive for all older cases, and that the State should advance compensation for all victims of violence, including those who do not receive compensation from the Office for Victim Compensation. It is in this country that laws do not normally have retroactive effect. When this was done after 22 July 2011, it was because one would not treat the terrorists and other victims of violence differently. As the Storting then saw it, it would have been unfortunate. Therefore, this last change had an impact on the whole of 2011. The retroactive effect should be used with great caution and with the shortest possible periods. It will also be contrary to previous legislative practices and will be able to put pressure on many areas in the future. The maximum optional victim compensation has been changed many times without retroactive effect. This may seem unimaginable to many, and it was probably noticeable in 2001, as the upper limit increased from DKK 200,000 to 20 G. In 2009, the upper limit increased to 40 G, so in 2011 the upper limit was increased to 60 G. It is not favorable for anyone to open old and resolved matters every time a law change is accepted. I also want to remind you that there was a lot of agreement last year to follow the general principle that laws are not being given retroactive effect, but that it is the time of damage that is decisive. I don't see the need to solve this question now, as Right wants. That question was given wide coverage last year in connection with the treatment of Prop. 65 L for 2011-2012. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e3867	It is important to ensure compensation for victims of violence and to make the system as fair as possible, as proposed by the proposers. That is why the Stoltenberg II Government has substantially amended the Act on the Compensation of Victims three times and strengthened the rights of the victims. I see proposal No. 1 as a wish that the limit of 60 G and all other provisions of the Act on the Compensation of Victims be given unlimited retroactive effect. The clear starting point is that laws do not give retroactive effect. Prior to the raising of the ceiling on 30 March 2012, the issue of retroactive effect was carefully assessed by the Ministry of Justice and Defence and then debated here in the United Kingdom. If the provision were to take effect from 1975, very many cases would have to be reopened. In the majority of cases, there is no judgment, and it would be difficult — often impossible — to obtain the necessary documentation to make the claims calculations. The legislative changes took effect on 1 January 2011. There were no cases dealt with after this date, where the then limit of 40 G had become significant. The victims’ compensation scheme has been amended on a number of points and it is difficult to identify issues for which the changes are relevant. If the provisions were to be applied retroactively, I would believe that all cases have to be reviewed since 1975. A large proportion of the money the state spends on victims of violence would have gone to proceedings over payments. I don't think there's a development that someone's earned. Furthermore, it would be difficult for the victims to move forward if the cases were reopened each time the law was amended. Indeed, the compensation scheme for victims is precisely based on the fact that the State pre-empts compensation for victims and takes over the claim for damages. I stress that a person who is awarded compensation from the perpetrator of a court will receive compensation for the victims of violence. In any case, in my view, there is no pressing need for a ban. Firstly, the issue seems to affect – as the representative Roppstad was about – very few people. Secondly, we have a law that facilitates practical solutions where there is a need to see people's faces to identify them. Furthermore, we have the opportunity to prohibit such clothing in specific situations, for example: in educational situations, and in cases where women are forced to wear durable garments, this will be affected by the provisions of the Criminal Code on coercion. I think the most important thing we can do to avoid women being pushed into using lucky clothes, or even choosing to use them, is to facilitate their inclusion in society in all areas. Overall, today we have legislation that allows prohibition in specific situations, which facilitates the implementation of the necessary ID control, and which allows us to find solutions based on mutual tolerance and respect more than a general prohibition.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e4003	Several have not asked for the floor on case No. 17.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e4017	At the request of the Judicial Committee, the President will propose that speaking time be limited to 24 minutes and allocated by up to 3 minutes to each party and up to 3 minutes to a member of the Government. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e4024	I would like to begin by commending the Christian People's Party for proposing changes that limit the growth of executive wages in limited liability companies. It is important that we turn all the stones in order to find ways to reduce top managers' wages, precisely because in recent years we have seen that the growth in management wages for some groups has been significantly higher than for employees. However, there is an overall committee which opposes the proposal to introduce binding shareholder voting on directors' remuneration in limited liability companies. Over the last few years, a significant simplification for Norwegian business has been carried out through the removal of the audit obligation, the reduction of share capital requirements for small and medium-sized enterprises and the simplification of the Share Act. It's important, when we impose new laws or tasks on business, that we ask ourselves: does this city want more work, more bureaucracy, and will this have huge costs for business? With regard to these proposals, I believe that the answer to both of these questions is yes. In addition to cost and additional work, it will address practical implementation challenges. In the case of companies with many shareholders, it would be impractical to convene the general meeting each time one is required to employ a person with a leading position in the company. The Committee considers that the proposal goes too far in restricting the company's freedom of action. It is important that the Governing Board has the necessary room for action and flexibility to negotiate pay and employment conditions which are deemed necessary to get the right person in place and that the recruitment process does not take too long. The second paragraph of Section 6-2 of the Share Act states that the Executive Director is employed by the Board of Directors, unless the statutes of the company state that this is to be done by the general meeting. The proposal of the Christian People's Party raises a fundamental question of competence and responsibility between the board and the general meeting. The Management Board is responsible for the management of the company and under the law responsible for ensuring the proper organisation of the business. The general meeting may lay down general guidelines for employment and it shall be able to give instructions on pay conditions. Furthermore, the Share Act does not have specific rules for the addition of other senior executives. It is governed by the general organisational rules of the law. This is why we cannot support the proposal, even though it itself has good intentions.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e4045	Businessman Thomas Minder won a referendum in Switzerland to introduce a binding shareholder vote on directors' salaries in the country's limited liability company. 68 pst. by the voters of the conservative and nutritious Switzerland said yes to Minder’s proposal. Pension funds are required to vote in the interests of their members and to publish their voting rights. The background to the proposal is the trend towards increasingly higher management salaries. These wage levels are increasingly less legitimate in the population, both in Switzerland and internationally. The conclusion of Minder’s proposal was not diminished by the decision by the pharmaceutical company Novartis this winter to provide Chairman Daniel Vasella with a final package of 430 million. kr. The vote will take place at international level. Several countries are now considering the introduction of binding shareholder voting on management salaries. We should also do that here in Norway. The wages of Norwegian business leaders are quite far from the levels of Switzerland or the OECD average. But there has also been a strong growth in management wages: they rose twice as quickly as usual in the decade up to 2007, according to Iver Bragelien, first amannuensis at the Norwegian School of Commerce. The second major trend in the last 20 years, including in Norway, is an increasingly widespread use of variable wage components as bonuses. The growth of management pay is worrying for all those who want to avoid a society with greater differences. We have an ever-increasing pay gap between workers in Norway and the rest of the world because of our petroleum-powered economy. Therefore, it is more important than ever to avoid strong growth in management wages that could undermine the idea of moderate wage-fixing and weaken the competitiveness of Norwegian enterprises abroad. Today, remuneration of directors of Norwegian limited liability companies is determined by the general meeting, while remuneration and remuneration for senior executives are determined by the board. The proposers believe that shareholders should also be directly influenced by the salaries and remuneration of senior executives. By shifting the final approval of salaries out of the narrow circle of directors and into the general meetings, shareholders are given increased influence and the growth of management salaries can be reduced. Strengthening the power of the stock is also really in principle. The shareholders own the company. The company’s board and administrative management should serve them, not the opposite. The British news magazine The Economist supports the proposal and believes it makes sense, precisely because it strengthens shareholders' influence. So here, the self-declared liberals in The Economist are more progressive than the red-green parties in this room, who wash their hands and seem to be satisfied with the leadership pay gap. That should give the red-green representatives reason for reflection. I might have to bring up the suggestions.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e4315	It's a great pleasure for me to present the setting of the maritime food message, the world's leading maritime union, to the Size. The message is good and was well received by the industry. We've done it even better through landfill treatment. This I will come back to. Ever since the first speed movement began well 10,000 years ago, adaptation to marine resources, especially fishing, has been the determining factor for the pattern of settlement, employment and economic development along the coast. In this way fisheries have adapted to time-consuming natural fluctuations, but with the massive technological development of the national and international fishing fleets after the 1900s, the main challenge was soon to adapt and regulate the take-offs in relation to natural resources. This has been, and is still, very demanding and complicated processes. I would like to stress that fishing resources and other marine resources are the property of the community, and the Marine Resource Act establishes the importance of ensuring sustainable and socio-economic management, which in turn will ensure employment and coastal settlement. With our marine and coastal areas, Norway has a particularly natural advantage compared to other nations. The natural advantages give us particular responsibility for producing food, feed for farming and other important inputs based on marine biological resources. In addition, with its strong position in the major maritime industries, fisheries, shipping, oil and gas, Norway has a unique position and the ability to be world leaders in marine technology, competence and value creation. Norway shall have ambitions to be a world-leading port, and we politicians shall facilitate this through good framework conditions and ensure that we manage resources in a sustainable way. We are very pleased that our Minister of Fisheries and Coast announced early on that she would promote a maritime food report for the Storting, and that Norway, as the world's leading maritime nation, is not just a vision, but a realistic and correct goal. It also shows this message. 2013 is the year when the government made up the status and cancelled the course for the future. Today, we adopt a long-term policy that sets the framework for Norwegian seafood in the coming years. The Storting Communication describes how Norway can develop as a maritime nation. Norwegian fjords and seas are incredible tax chambers. There are a number of resources that are used to a small extent and that can give rise to new marine value creation. I am pleased that in the treatment there seems to be a great deal of agreement to facilitate new marine growth. In several areas, Norway is already one of the world’s leading maritime states. We are the world's second largest exporter of seafood. Our management of fisheries and aquaculture is one of the world's leading competences and management. Many countries look to us with wonder and wonder how we get it. Several key reports and strategies point to the industrial strength and growth potential of marine industries. Common to several of the reports is the view that Norwegian seafood will play an important national and international role. The seafood industry plays an important role in developing Norway as the world’s leading maritime nation. However, the profitability of the agricultural industry has been weak for a long time, especially in the fillet industry. There may be a lot of reasons for that. The production of ready-to-use products and direct contact with the market are essential for a dynamic seafood industry that is to achieve the highest possible value creation based on our marine resources. At the same time, the seafood industry operates in markets characterised by strong competition in both the raw material and the finished market. We are therefore pleased that the industry has gained a central role in the maritime food communication. I would like to mention in particular a few of the measures. One is the establishment of a public sample to review the framework conditions of the marine food industry by the end of 2014. The second is that the research funds should be targeted at market, technology and product development as well as better use of the residual raw material. Developing a more competitive seafood industry while ensuring that value creation and jobs benefit coastal communities is a challenge. 95 % of all seafood produced in Norway is exported. It is therefore important that the government has an active policy to ensure Norwegian maritime food market access in international markets. Market-oriented work is very important for Norwegian seafood to have a stable framework and to ensure that the industry can meet the demand for seafood from Norway. The Norwegian seafood industry has a good starting point with proximity to large fishing resources from fisheries and aquaculture combined with a short path to a well-paid market in Europe and Russia. However, if Norway is to be the best at sea food, the quality of raw material must be world-class. It's not like that today. The fishermen here have to take a much greater responsibility even for the quality of the raw material. We support the proposal to strengthen the supervision of raw material quality and raw material processing under the supervision of the fish sales laws. I also notice that the Progress Party is against this, unfortunately. Investments in innovation and technology development are important to ensure a profitable rural industry in the future. The industrial policy instrument plays an important role in this regard, as do schemes such as SkattenFUNN, which the Government strengthens in the revised national budget. It is a policy objective to maintain a varied coastal fishing fleet and to facilitate controlled structuring in line with the need for increased income. The adaptations shall be within the framework of a structured policy designed to achieve fisheries policy objectives. In the maritime food notification, the government proposes to adjust the quota ceiling for the fleet over 11 metres and to facilitate a greater degree of specialisation. We received clear feedback in the hearing that fishermen themselves wanted freedom of choice when it came to quota composition by structuring. We have listened to this, and together with the Ministry of Fisheries and the Coast, we have come up with a unified solution, with which I record that the industry is now well pleased. Based on an overall assessment, the structural quota scheme for the fleet is not introduced below 11 metres. The issue of quota access and profitability will be addressed. Meanwhile, the temporary co-fishing system will be continued. We support the proposal not to change the quota year and to consider the issue of quota flexibility. This can be a good measure to secure raw materials for industry. It is also very good that the health perspective is raised and that it focuses on ensuring high-quality and safe food for consumers. Investment in knowledge is a red thread throughout the message. The government will strengthen the publicly funded marine research, and I notice that this is something it seems to be a cross-political consensus on. We support the government's overall objective of further growth in the aquaculture industry within a sustainable framework. There is no doubt that the industry's main environmental challenges in the short term are related to escape and leachus, and the impact this may have on the wild. It is therefore proposed to establish methods for monitoring and measuring the impact of escape and salmonids. When limit values are exceeded, measures shall be considered. This shall provide management with better opportunities to regulate the aquaculture industry in an environmentally sustainable manner. In the long term, land use is likely to be the main challenge for industry. It is therefore appropriate to develop a zone-based land structure in the aquaculture sector. It is important that the interests of small and medium-sized enterprises are properly taken into account. In addition, it is important that municipalities receive part of the remuneration for the granting of new permits – this in order to give municipalities an incentive to plan for aquaculture. As a summary: this is an important and good maritime food message with many good measures that I record that the opposition is also largely behind it. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e4533	There is a broad consensus today that the seafood industry is one of the most important export industries we have. This is also the case with the Right Seafood Communication. Openness about politics is a good thing, we believe in the Right. I record that you think we're late, and that you're impatient in position to see what we're doing. I'll take that as a good sign. That's a healthy interest, and our message should come. What does the Right mean about the government's message? We believe that there is a message that contains important descriptions of the total seafood industry. It extends the concept of maritime nutrition to also include new fields where we are not yet completely at the top of the world, but where the potential is great. That's good. In the right, we believe that it is an important point that this is now more viewed as one industry, even though it is fragmented in several layers – with coastal fishing teams, fishing teams, Pelagic Association and fishing boats on the sea side, and with FHL and NSL on land. This is probably from a democratic point of view – and freedom of organisation is a value it is important to fight for – but for the industry it is important that it is perceived collectively. An overall seafood diet, and the seafood industry as a concept, is becoming important in the future, and it has been well treated in the message that has now come. The reason for this is that the industry is going to compete – on areas, resources, personnel, research resources, capital, infrastructure resources, etc. In fact, in order to exemplify, it is that there is more weight on the argument if the seismic shooting takes place at the expense of the seafood industry than if it were a problem for five flyers. So the maritime food notification should give us a brief summary of a policy that facilitates the development of the marine food industry nationally. One of our three most important knowledge clusters is to grow several times, so that Norway, as a nation, is to reach the vision of ‘the world’s foremost maritime state’. Where do we separate our paths? As in the Agriculture Communication, this message is full of objectives, but not as crystal clear on how to achieve these objectives. The seafood industry is, as has been said before, a relatively well-regulated industry. For the right, it is important that industrial policies facilitate growth and profitability, and not least that we focus on opportunities for growth and profitable operations. Therefore, predictability and the possibility of long-term planning are important factors for us. We believe that the government in the communication still sows confusion as to the framework factors that business operators can see for themselves in the future. What are the structural models to be applied? Up to the middle of the committee procedure, one model has been delivered, then the industry has turned around and now presents the flexibility that the industry wants. This isn't something you've been told about in the last 14 days, so you control, but you control the wings. How does growth in the aquaculture industry regulate? What should govern how much ownership you can occupy in both fleet, industry and aquaculture? In all these areas, predictable framework conditions will be important if the industry is to grow as much as we want it to. The right considers that the marine food industry, as the global industry we describe it, needs to have the market more as a guide. Existing markets must be maintained and new markets must be developed. To succeed, the correct quality of raw material must be available when required by the market. This applies to both white fish, aquaculture and pelagic industries. It is, therefore, the policy that needs to be implemented so that this can be done. The proposed changes to the consultation note on delivery obligations, for example, could, unfortunately, lead to a deterioration rather than an improvement, since no account is taken of what the market will have, but add up to a regulation that can quickly lead to an increase in the problem of overfish on land in the first four months of the year. This will allow a review of a number of regulations in the maritime food industry – it will be regulated away from market-oriented operations rather than tending to the market when it is there. If we're going to reach the vision, we're going to have to do more research. We have to do things smarter than our competitors because of a high level of costs and a demanding competitive situation. I therefore refer to our remarks, where in the Right we have a significantly higher investment in research than the government parties. Our policy also facilitates more research initiated by the companies themselves. This is, in our opinion, very important, and the key if we are to succeed in commercialising more of our research efforts: close cooperation between the research environments and a business with research focus. I record that representative Hansen is bringing forward SkattenFUNN as the new tool, on behalf of the government. There I have to be allowed to arrest the representative. Actually, it's not like you've improved the Tax Fund. You've basically tightened it up, and then you've announced an improvement, but you haven't delivered it. It can come to the harvest. It was notified in the research report and it is announced in this message, but it has not been delivered. It is experientially easy to wait for things to be delivered before cheering. In the Right, we believe that more of the value creation must belong directly to the communities. This is to give incentives to facilitate activity. We have therefore proposed that a greater proportion of the corporate tax should apply directly to the municipalities. In addition, the concession fee and the tax on MTB should apply to the municipalities concerned. Right view predictable growth as a good tool for the industry to research more and develop both more products and more markets. We also believe that the demand for sustainable growth is best achieved by facilitating predictable growth. We also suspend sustainability by stating the principle that each polluter pays. In the right we don't believe that a solidarity fund is the right way to go. We believe that the responsible breeder must also take the load when catching up and cleaning up. This seems more preventative in our opinion, and makes higher demands on individuals to meet the sustainability criteria. In summary, we have described three important policy principles in the notes. Policies shall primarily facilitate, not engage in business and value creation. The fact that more of the value creation belongs to the community in question is about the distribution of power. The right builds policy from the bottom to the top, and then provides a framework that allows local communities to facilitate the value creation of all kinds. Long-termity and predictable frameworks provide robust growth conditions, which will be necessary if we are to reach the vision of becoming the world's foremost maritime state.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e4639	Norway has natural premises to be the world's foremost maritime nation. We have a long coastline, and the continental shelf off the coast is an important spawning and breeding area. According to the Marine Research Institute, 70 pst. of all the fish in the Norwegian Sea and in the Barents Sea spawning area or growing area on the narrow continental shelf outside the Lofoten and the Western Sea. Fishing resources and other marine resources are jointly owned. The fishing resources are renewable resources when we manage them effectively. A Joint Committee therefore emphasises the precautionary principle and ecosystem approach in fisheries management. Clean, rich and productive ecosystems are a means of achieving the goal of becoming the world's foremost maritime state. Marine resources laws stipulate that marine wild resources shall contribute to ensuring employment and coastal communities. SV is committed to pursuing a policy that takes account of this objective. Coastal fishing fleets, and not least the smallest fleet, are important for many communities, which also Right just admitted. The group under 11 meters is important for the bus ride along the coast. She is also important for recruiting for the fishing profession. Therefore, the government does not propose structuring in the group below 11 meters. There are different views of the economy of this group. Therefore, SV is pleased that it is proposed to dispose of the economy in the group. SV is committed to strengthening the economy without structuring. The world's foremost maritime capacity must also be at the forefront when it comes to marine research. This is an area where government priorities both in the maritime food message and in the research message. As we develop better fishing methods, it is important to have good knowledge of ecosystems. We also need research that can reduce costs both in the fisheries and in the fishing industry and that can increase market prices. Breeding industries have grown rapidly, and industry faces challenges in relation to environmental sustainability. It can have major consequences for the wild and other parts of the ecosystem if we don't take these challenges seriously. It can also destroy the economic power of the industry. Therefore, it is positive that a Joint Committee notes that further growth in the industry depends on addressing the environmental challenges and that one also addresses the challenges of feed in a resilient way. This is a ratio that has been updated since today's media look-up. Here it's natural to go over to talking about aquaculture laws. If we are to achieve the goal of the maritime food communication, it is important to have laws and regulations that can contribute to the environmental sustainability of the farming industry. It is important for the environment and for the industry itself. All experience from other industries shows that strict environmental requirements create innovation that benefits industry. Aquaculture law entered into force on 1 January 2006. It is now time to update some of the laws based on experience so far. Here, as often happens, prevention is the best. The government has proposed to amend the second paragraph of Section 10 so that the requirement to carry out preventive environmental work becomes clearer. There is something SV is busy about. Prevention is always the best environmental work – better than repair in retrospect. The Government also proposes to amend the second paragraph of Section 10 in order to allow the Ministry to introduce requirements for the labelling of fish. The aim is to better control the incorporation of escaped farmed fish into the river, and to trace them back to the farm – when we get adequate methods for it. It is important that in the further work on labelling, an emphasis is placed on animal health and good contact with the industry, to which a single committee has focused. The government also proposes to amend Section 11 in order to allow farmers to carry out or finance the necessary environmental research within their area of influence. The government also proposes to introduce an economic joint responsibility for the removal of escaped farmed salmon in the river. It's important and right that the industry cleans up afterwards. The Christian People's Party proposes to make the payments depending on the risk of escape in the plant. The intention may be good, but it may be difficult to find a fair practice of such differentiation. Finally, the goal of becoming the world's leading seamate is ambitious but realistic. We can achieve this by focusing on knowledge and management – for environmental and resource resilience.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e4712	The history of Norwegian seafood is the story of how a thousand-year tradition of harvesting the sea's rich resources has laid the basis for Norway's being a major act of marine food today. Over 150 countries import wild caught and farmed fish from Norway. In all, Norway exports a total of 36 million seafood meals every day. In 2012, the export value amounted to a total of 51.6 billion. kr, which is Norway's third largest export industry. This is an industry for the future, and the choices we are making now will be crucial for both the industry and our viable coastal communities. The centre party is therefore satisfied with the government’s great vision in the maritime food announcement that Norway will be the world’s leading maritime nation. The centre party is committed to facilitating a forward-looking maritime food industry — an industry with a high potential for coastal development. It is therefore important that the notification covers the whole value chain, from primary to market. The government is committed to strengthening Norway’s already strong position as a seamat on the global market, thereby taking responsibility for increasing food supply and strengthening food security in a world of high population growth. To cope with this, we need to be knowledgeable in areas such as sustainable resource use, marine environment, climate, product development, quality and market. In the future, particular attention will be paid to market and marketing. At the same time, the Centre Party and the Government are committed to bringing maritime food to the benefit of the coast and the coastal population and contributing to the creation of viable coastal communities. For the Central Party, this perspective is crucial. Sustainable resource management is essential to ensure a viable maritime food industry. The Central Party and the Government want fisheries and aquaculture policy to be designed to help ensure profitable farms both on the sea and on land, and to achieve rural policy objectives such as jobs and settlement in coastal communities. The centre party is satisfied that the maritime feed notification determines that it will facilitate growth in the aquaculture industry. This industry has a high potential for growth and can become Norway’s new adventures at sea. We are also very concerned that it should facilitate a varied structure in the industry. The centre party is particularly concerned with the locally anchored companies, with many small and medium-sized enterprises – this is because, in particular, these enterprises are important community builders in their communities. The centre party is also satisfied that the notification announces that municipalities will receive part of the financial compensation from new concessions in the aquaculture industry. With this measure, municipalities are getting more back to put their areas at the disposal of the aquaculture industry. The centre batch is satisfied that the notification provides for a future spatial structure that takes into account small and medium-sized operators in the farming industry and takes into account the experience of local and voluntary work. The centre lot will underline the importance of this work when further arrangements are to be made for production areas, zones and fire holes. This means that the model launched in the Land Selection, or the so-called Gullestad selection, is not implemented. The centre will stress that important and coordinated work is already being done in the industry – with synchronised postponement and set-aside – and that it is important to build on the experience gained. We will also underline the importance of local and regional coastal zone planning. In this context, good interaction between the municipalities of the different regions and the county municipality is very important. Sustainable development is important for the aquaculture industry, and it is therefore of great importance that the industry continues to work on the challenges it faces in terms of lice and escape. These are areas that they need to pay a lot of attention in the future. The fishing industry is the basis for settlement in many coastal communities, and the Centre Party is committed to strengthening the fishing industry by ensuring a varied, profitable and forward-looking fleet that provides the basis for the recruitment and renewal of the coastal fleet. The centre party has always had a cautious approach to structure, but supports the government's proposal to adjust the quota base within the individual fleet groups, with a view to strengthening the profitability of the coastal fleet – this in order to clean up the various arrangements we have in the industry today for the ship groups above 11 metres, in order to ensure as much equality as possible. That's why we're very pleased that we're now getting an optional structure for the fleet over 11 meters. The group of vessels of less than 11 metres shall be allowed to continue as before – without structure – including the co-fishing system. We're satisfied with that in the Center Party. A separate study on quota access and profitability shall also be carried out for this group. Let me also stress that we call about the principles of the Raw Fish Act and the Participation Act, to which we come back. The seafood industry will have its own investigation in the future. That is very important. Seafood notification has been very well received along the coast. It shows that the red-green government is offensively betting to reach the vision of Norway as the world's leading maritime nation.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e4811	The very title of the government maritime food communication is a Memorandum of Understanding with broad cross-political support. Our common vision is that Norway is going to be the world's leading maritime nation now and in the future. According to a report prepared by the government, Norwegian maritime food industry will have an economic sales potential of a total of 550 billion. NOK 2050, i.e. a six-fold increase in relation to today. The big question is: how can industry in dialogue and cooperation with political authorities achieve such an ambitious goal? Let me begin by stressing that the Christian People's Party is behind the vision of continued growth in all sectors of Norwegian seafood. At the same time, based on our ideological anchoring, we emphasise the principle of active management responsibility in terms of resources and the environment. We therefore note with satisfaction that the maritime communication contains a full chapter on sustainable management of natural resources. The Government assumes that the maritime food industry will continue to develop within the framework of social, economic and environmental sustainability. It is also underlined that the management of the marine food industry should be based on the principle of precautionary approach and ecosystem approach. These are strong, clear and important signals from the government that the Christian People's Party will support. In our practical policies, we will also do our utmost to ensure that these words are filled with content when political authorities set a framework for the administration of Norwegian seafood. In a brief article, it is not possible to address all aspects of the development of Norwegian seafood as described in the Maritime Food Communication. I must therefore confine myself to some matters that have been important to the Christian People's Party in its work on the processing of the message. The issue of the arrangement of the coastal-fleet quota system was raised in the context of the submission of the maritime feed notification. The government initially signaled an increase in the quota ceiling both for vessels over 15 metres and for vessels from 11 metres to 15 metres. At the same time, clear signals were given that the government wants increased specialisation for individual vessels in either the pelagic sector or the cod sector once the quota ceiling has been reached. The Government therefore expressed its objective of introducing a 4+2 quota allocation for vessels over 15 metres and 3+1 in the vessel group from 11 to 15 metres. We in the Christian People's Party have noticed that in their consultation opinions on the maritime food communication, Norway's Fish Team strongly argued against the fact that the coastal fishing fleet was to be forced into a system of increased specialisation. The Christian People's Party therefore called for voluntary action at an early stage for each vessel as regards quota allocation between the pelagic sector and the cod sector. Today, together with the Progress Party and the Right, we are promoting proposals to ask the government to ensure such freedom of choice by allowing the owner of vessels over 15 metres and the owner of vessels of categories 11 to 15 metres to choose an allocation of 3+3 or 2+2 within the quota ceiling, respectively. At the same time, we are pleased to see that all the parties – including the government parties – gradually concluded on what has always been the primary position of the Christian People's Party. This is a very important issue for coastal fishermen. It should therefore be a pleasure for the entire fishing industry to see politicians in all parties being willing to listen to factual and good arguments from fishermen’s own organisations. The willingness of the government to move as far as possible to adjust its own policy towards the recently issued notification also indicates flexibility and good listening ability. That is why we want to interpret the change in the course of the government positively in this matter. But there are also the benefits of the Christian People’s Party to the government. The maritime notification also contains a chapter on aquaculture, where the government draws up the lines for future framework conditions for the farming industry. Here it is several to put a finger on in the government's descriptions of environmental measures and balances between industrial and environmental considerations. We'll get back to that in a later article. Finally, I will mention an important situation in which the Christian People's Party appears to be standing alone with its position. All the batches in Stortinget will provide some compensation to the municipalities for the establishment of farming facilities. The majority want this to be a one-off remuneration in the form of part of the concession fee. On the other hand, the Christian People's Party proposes a model similar to that envisaged by the fjord and coastal network municipalities in its consultation opinion, namely an annual fee calculated on the basis of the area of the farms. We then assume that this should not lead to an increase in the costs for the breeding companies, but that the area tax should be deductible by offsetting the tax. On behalf of the Christian People's Party I promote the proposals No. One, two, six.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e4877	This is one day I've been looking forward to, and the jam became fish in all the canals today – although it might not have been exactly the seafood message that went back in today's news picture. We have a long and rich history as a seamate, and today's debate is about how we can manage this proud history and take Norway further as the world's leading maritime nation. With the maritime food message, we take the choices that make it possible to realise the enormous potential outlined for this industry. We got it communicated through the report that said we can reach a turnover in the industry of 550 billion. NOK by 2050, in Norway. Obviously, it's going to be if we take in the growth of the world's population. At the same time, we know that much of the land's potential agricultural area is used, while only barely 2 pst. of the food resources come from the sea, which actually amounts to 70 pst. of the globe. Kofi Annan said when he visited the AquaVision conference in Stavanger: «I have little knowledge of aquaculture, but I can so much about hunger». His message to us was clear. It was not primarily the fish he wanted us to contribute, but the knowledge. Science, research, innovation and development are a red thread through the Communication. We are quite clear that we need to strengthen marine research. Annually, we're using 3 billion. kr on marine research. The public accounts for a large part of marine research, with over 60 pst. funded by the authorities. We need to strengthen the public sector, but the industry must also be involved. That is why I have proposed to increase the research fee, which is in practice a large layer of sprawl from different parts of the industry, and to allow it to also be used for measures that increase the overall competence of the industry. Investing in and further developing and developing the enormous knowledge that fishermen, breeders and others have, is one of the best investments we can make. It is precisely the close interaction between the seafood industry and the supplier industry that has made it possible for us to provide equipment for fishing and farming worldwide, and we – the government – will build up during this interaction. Two years ago, I asked some of Norway's best environments of competence at the intersection of research and industrial development to collect input from the research communities and industry – and advise us on how we should strategically address research efforts in the marine areas. Their work was addressed in the report ‘HAV21 – a port of formats’. The report will be followed up in the Marine knowledge promise action plan. We hope to be able to present the action plan as early as autumn. Another carrier in the Storing Message is profitable. The debate on the structure of the fishing fleet illustrates the interaction between politics and regulation in an industry where we reap the life of the sea. Stronger investment in research has given us increased knowledge of marine populations. Regulation and strategic efforts to cooperate with our neighbouring countries on resource management have contributed to solid populations. Nature, however, limits what we can take out. On the other hand, technological development and improved methods have enabled fishermen to operate more efficiently and with fewer crews. If fishermen are to have incomes that provide profitable operation, and not least the means to invest, income must follow the development of prices and wages in society. This is important for recruitment. In the maritime food notification we open up so that the fishermen can gather several fish quotas on one boat. That means there will be fewer boats and fewer fishermen. Some ask if it's negative. To that is the answer that it is not a goal in itself to have as many fishermen as possible. But it is absolutely an important goal that people will become fishermen – that it is seen as an attractive profession, that it provides a good income to live off, and that fishermen, like farm owners, can invest in their business. Fishermen shall also be able to combine fishing with a life on land. Then we also have to give them the opportunity to increase their profitability and to drive more together on one boat. The latter has to do with increased security. On this issue, we have been very busy having dialogue with the fishermen themselves, and I would like to take the opportunity to praise the Norwegian Fisheries Team for the good cooperation we have had on this and on other issues. Profitability is also crucial for industry — the land part of the marine food industry. It is challenging to run fish processing in a country with high compensation of employees, but it is absolutely possible. The government has an ambition that we will continue processing more of the fish in Norway. It's not only because we want the jobs that the process itself provides – that's important enough – but we send fish out of the country without processing, we not only miss the value creation, but also the residual raw material and the opportunities it provides for new knowledge-based business. The world's foremost seamath must also have the ambition to be the best in the world on marine oils, proteins and other components. Let me say that sustainability is a starting point for everything we do – especially environmental sustainability, although the other two components of sustainability are also important. In the Single European Act, we propose important measures on the environment to help the maritime food industry continue to be an environmental industry. The changes we propose in the Aquaculture Act are a lot about strengthening the environmental chapter in the law. The supplier industry is given an important place – an area with a high potential. There are many framework conditions that politicians set for the industry. The most important thing for an export-oriented industry, despite the fact that I believe the message we are discussing here today is the world's most important, I still have to admit that a secure economic policy is more important. I must therefore take this opportunity to stress that the situation for parts of the industry – especially in the whitefish industry – is demanding. This situation comes, I'm afraid, to persist for a while, and then this is not the time for economic gambling, and I will warn against the Progress Party's approach to the rule of action. I am a defender of a tight economic policy – not because I don't want more roads in the districts, but because the consequence of irresponsible use of money can quickly be that there is no longer a fishing reception at the end of the same road. A few weeks ago, a group of committed students in the field of chemical process technology at NTNU sent me a project work. The project was about macroalgae, or many in good Norwegian would call it seaweed. They point to potential uses such as human food, animal feed and, in particular, biofuels. If biofuels from macroalgae are an alternative to biofuels produced on possible food soils, ask the students. I think that's a good question. I do not have the answer – that is why I want more people to ask such questions and want to find answers. The potential to increase the production from the sea is perhaps greater than we are now fully able to understand. With this big message, we are preparing the reason why we can become “the world’s foremost maritime nation”.

We have a cell stock that is completely out of control, where we have to bring down the stock, precisely because of the amount of fish that the selenium eats. In this context, it's a little strange that we haven't found room to take that debate here. The different predators affect each other, and it's clear that if the whale population and the cell population continue to grow, it will eventually go beyond the quotas and what we can take out of biomass on our different fish stocks. It's worrying when we see the number of cell cuts going into the ice to catch. It's worrying when we see the population growing – we remember a few years back in time, when we got a selinvation along the coast. I am afraid that we can get one of these situations again, where our fjord fishermen will not be able to sell their products because of illness and crosshairs. It is very strange that we have not taken the time to ensure that this is addressed in this debate. It is just as much the responsibility of the Progress Party that we have not submitted any comments on this. That's why I chose to use this post to talk about the importance of making sure that we keep up these populations, which affect each other. Then we have to make sure that the receiver for the harness is in place. Then we have to make sure that our whalers have the framework conditions to be able to catch what they're going to do. Then we have to make sure that the legislation relating to whether you enter or leave a zone to catch the wave wave wave is of a more or less academic nature related to these regulations, when we know that it is the same stock. These things we have to take with us, and we have to expect it to be caught better in the future.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e5271	Few, if any, debates create more temperature, commitment and bulky posts in fisheries circles than those that have the raw fish law as their theme. The law is referred to as the fisherman's basic law. It once gave fishermen a monopoly on catching and first-hand placing on the market of fish. In addition, there was an opening in the law to allow fishermen themselves to set the minimum price. This was intended as a safety network and to increase predictability in operation. Seafood is currently a global industry where market price transparency is quite different from 75 years ago. The possibilities of transporting raw materials also give fishermen a completely different negotiating position today. The last and most important understanding in this picture is that it's probably more what the market is willing to pay, which determines what the price down the value chain is. This understanding becomes important to work ahead of time. The new Fish Salmon Law is versatile. It entitles fishermen in law to catch and first-hand trade of fish, including the choice of selling form, and the Minimum Price Institute. However, in addition to this, it imposes a number of obligations on fishermen via its sales teams. The law obliges fishermen to acquire and avail themselves of adequate market competence. The raw fish layer manages the market competence of the fish. It obliges pricing to take account of the next stages of the value chain so that profitability can be achieved at all stages. It is therefore also the responsibility of the workers on land to have a viable framework. I'll make it short, since it's late in the evening. It is that the aim of the law goes a little parallel to the great vision: a well paying consumer who chooses Norwegian seafood products because they have the best quality, is simple to prepare and submit confidence that they are the catch of sustainable stocks. When we get there, we've come to where we want to go. On behalf of the Progress Party, I am promoting their proposals. And then I just want to add briefly that the matter is promoted by an overall committee.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e5384	The government has put forward a bill on new law on the first-hand marketing of wild marine resources. The main features of the Raw Fish Act have been passed, but the proposal implies that we have a new and modern law harmonised with the other two central laws applicable to the fishing industry. The new purpose paragraph expands the purpose of the law by emphasising the involvement of the law in sustainable and socially viable management. The law has a larger dimension than just providing good and stable prices for fishermen. It shall include, inter alia: ensure a fair distribution of revenue from the market between fishermen and industry. This will ensure a stable framework for turnover, which benefits both the fishermen’s side and the industry’s and fleet’s side. In addition, the social responsibility of the sales teams is highlighted by their control of the resource outsourcing. The legislative proposal provides for the setting of minimum prices and the procedure to be followed in the event of disagreement between the parties. The sales teams have, as before, given the last word, if no agreement is reached, but only after mediation has been completed. I feel that a lot of the debate has just been about setting the minimum price. I stress that the law contains more than that. I recognise that it is important, but I do believe that the normal thing – and this has been true in most cases for many years – is that the parties have agreed to negotiations. Then the almost question has to be whether the representative Nesvik is dissatisfied with the minimum price, because we are really talking about setting the minimum price and not what is possible to get into a market. Finally, I would like to mention that the new law allows the sales laws to impose controls on the quality of raw materials. The aim is to contribute to improving the quality of the catch delivered. In my view, the legislative proposal will provide an important update of the legislation on the first-hand placing on the market of wild marine resources.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e5404	At the beginning of the sample work and at the hearing, some criticisms were made that the law was not fully reviewed when it was first started. The term of office was limited and was further restricted after the sample manager asked for clarification. In the opinion of the Right, it is little luck that you did not attack this case as widely as possible when you were going to have a review. A more thorough examination of the role of the market and a stronger emphasis on the principle of objectivity in the sales strike mechanism would, in the Right’s view, be desirable. It wasn't done. Of course, one can inquisitorially claim that if this is Right's view of the matter, the Minimum Price Institute falls with Right in government. No, that's actually not right. In the new Fish Sale Act, the right of the model with mediation is also possible in case of disagreement on pricing, and that fishermen get the last word after mediation. Why this contradiction? The new Fish Sale Act is versatile. It entitles fishermen in law to catch and first-hand trade of fish, including the choice of selling form, and the Minimum Price Institute. But in addition to this, it imposes a number of obligations on fishermen, via its sales teams. The law obliges fishermen to acquire and avail themselves of adequate market competence. The raw fish layer shall manage the market competence of the fish. It obliges pricing to take account of the next stages of the value chain so that profitability can be achieved at all stages. It is therefore also the responsibility of the workers on land to have a viable framework. It obliges fishermen to supply catches of such quality as to enable the industry to choose between several forms of production. It obliges fishermen in respect of resource control. Sustainability certification will be the driving force in many, many more markets in the future. If there's any doubt about whether we report the right volume in resource management, we can face greater loss of reputation and values than we like to talk about. The reputation of the seafood industry must therefore be a shared responsibility – as clearly on the boat deck as in the production hall. In short, the new law makes greater demands on fishermen to see their role in a more comprehensive picture. In the Right, we believe that the new law, with the mediation agency, will have a disciplinary effect in terms of playing against their own and other actors in the industry. This will benefit both fishermen and industry. The right-hand objective of the new Fish Sales Act and the Storing Communication on Seafood is to start on the road to profitability at all stages. We think it is possible to achieve this by giving industry a more market-oriented approach to catch, a quality thinking that goes far beyond the needs of individual joints, and a production understanding and product development that provides both more sales and better prices in the market. A well-paying consumer who chooses Norwegian seafood products because they have the best quality, because they are simple to prepare, and because they provide confidence that they are caught and sustainable stocks, is a good image we're going to have in the debate further. Finally, I would like to add that the last winter's tumults and debates around the minimum price show that the raw fish layer was the task of growing up. In the storm they stood against both their own members and the industry, and in my opinion they did a good job. So that's also a little bit of the Right rationale for being conservative and changing slowly. We believe this is a good law.

At the same time, control activities will also help to ensure the legitimacy of the secret services. A liberal society must have functional secret services that work for the security of the state, combined with good control procedures. In 2012, the EOS Committee carried out 30 inspections, of which an unannounced check. The Committee notes that the EOS Committee has complied with the requirements of the control directive, but stresses that it must be considered as a minimum for the control activities, which means that the sample is completely free to carry out more controls. In addition, the EOS Committee itself has to assess the future use of unannounced controls.
