There were several registrations on persons based on their connection to the religious environment, without any additional elements underpinning the registration. It is contrary to the PST instruction, § 15. The Committee considers that it is important that the PST be closely associated with the legal frameworks laid down for the activities and the possibility of registering persons and the necessary procedures for following up registrations and deleting records for which there is no longer any basis. In the course of the work of the EOS Committee, the PST itself initiated a new review of these records, resulting in a large number of deletions from the PST register. It is necessary for the confidence of the PST to improve these procedures and to ensure that these records are within the regulatory limits. I would also like to add that it is an inherent challenge for the secret services that they, on the one hand, get criticism for not detecting or registering persons who can later be linked to acts of terrorism or preparatory actions, while on the other hand, get criticism for registering too many. It is a challenging balancing exercise that the service has to live daily. Finally, some brief – that is to say fast – words about the proposal for a representative, Document 8:130 L for 2012–2013, where all parties – and it is very pleasing – agree that the right of access of the EOS Committee to telecommunications companies providing services for the secret services should apply universally. Today, access to the control of the EOS Committee is limited to the companies owned by the country itself. In practice, there has been Telenor, while they have not had the ability to control the other telecommunications companies. With this amendment, the right of access to the EOS Committee is made universal, regardless of who owns the telecommunications provider. It's important, and that's good. ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e5583	It is of great importance that citizens have confidence in the secret services and that they do not go beyond the laws and instructions that apply. Here, as I said, it was discovered that the Intelligence Battalion had stored information about journalists — name, image, education and work history from published news articles — which the selection concluded that the Intelligence Battalion had no legal basis for. This shows the significance of the sample studies, and an overall committee takes the view that the EOS Committee regularly checks the relevant units of the defence, in the same line and with the same rights and powers as they have vis-à-vis the other E-service. So a few words about the EOS Committee's special message to the Storting regarding PST's registration of persons associated with two Muslim environments. It is important to clarify that the PST has as one of its most important tasks the prevention of events that could undermine national security and damage vital social interests. In this context, the PST has the power to monitor persons where the service finds grounds to investigate whether someone is preparing for a criminal offence, a criminal offence, which the PST has the task of preventing. But there are also clear limitations here. The PST instruction § 15 does not provide for the opening of the registration of persons only on the basis of their political or religious convictions. This provision limits the persons to whom the service has legal basis for monitoring in the light of prevention. Thus, the EOS Committee's studies concern the monitoring of two Muslim environments by PST, and the purpose was to see whether this could be problematic in relation to the PST instruction § 15. Now it is said that the EOS Committee determines that the PST initially had a basis for monitoring central people related to this environment. The challenge in this case is that in addition, the PST has processed data on a dozen persons where the relationship to these environments has been remote and peripheral, at least so remote and peripheral that the requirements for registration do not appear to have been met. In this way, the service may have recorded information about these which has not been necessary for the service to follow. We are talking here about a number of people who are registered only on the basis of what is known about their religious beliefs, and where the registration violates the aforementioned section 15. The PST has been in touch with this and has put in place several measures to clean up registration practices, and that is good. The Christian People's Party believes it is important that the secret services are under democratic control and democratic control, and with which I started: the EOS Committee contributes through its work and investigations to creating trust, while the Stortinge gets useful information about the work of the secret services. In order to further improve the work and control function of the EOS Committee, the Christian People's Party is also involved in promoting a legislative amendment and clarification of Section 4 of the EOS Act so that the sample is also allowed to inspect private companies assisting the PST in its task execution.

ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e5657	I present a unanimous opinion from the Committee on Control and Constitutional Affairs. The National Audit Office’s Annual Report sets out the Riksaudition’s framework conditions, management system and achievement target for 2012. The Committee has noted and supported the fact that the Riksrevision vision is to contribute effectively to the control of the Storting and to promote good governance. It is in line with what I see as the role of the National Audit Office in Norwegian policy and in Norwegian administration. In a democratic society, control of administration is absolutely necessary. The National Audit Office is the largest external control body in the United Kingdom, and it is important for the Storting that there is a continuous review of the management's ability and willingness to follow up the decisions of the Storting – not least because, as elected representatives, we are to uphold the rights and demands of every citizen to know whether the resources are being used effectively and in line with political decisions. I am therefore pleased that a unanimous committee believes that the annual report shows that the National Audit Office is working hard and focused on this ambitious objective of effective control and better management. It is worth noting that the Riksrevision in various opinion polls scores very high on both credibility, integrity, professionalism and competence. Furthermore, the Committee has noted that the overall management seems to be satisfied with the work carried out by the Riksrevision. The user surveys have demonstrated that the administration has experienced a particularly good dialogue with the Riksrevision. It also shows that 82 pst. the administrative respondents experience that the Riksrevision has sufficient competence in the implementation of the tasks. However, there are – almost obviously – improvement points for the Riksrevision too. On two points, user surveys indicate that there are varying results for target achievement: good guidance and presentation of audit results. The Committee welcomes the fact that the Riksrevision itself expresses its commitment to improving these two points. In 2012, the Riksrevision sent 15 management reports to Stortinget. The reports show – albeit in slightly different ways – that management should be better adapted to the objectives and requirements of the enterprise or area. It is essential that the Riksrevision should be included in the reforms, development processes and plans undertaken at all times in the Norwegian administration. The Annual Report provides us with a good overview of a broad range of activities that are important for the Storting to receive information about in order to fulfil its responsibilities as a constitutional body. The Committee believes that it is important that the Riksrevision addresses issues that go across the ministry. A cross-sectoral perspective is both sensible and necessary, not least because policy areas are interrelated and because they affect each other. The National Audit Office has listed developments in government that will be significant in the future. It concerns the sustainability of the public welfare system, the economic framework and efficient use of resources in the country, municipal service production, health and care, social security and preparedness, environmental and climate challenges – to name a few. These are areas that concern us all and have a major impact on the individual's living conditions and the possibility of life development. The Committee therefore welcomes and agrees that the Riksrevision focuses its attention on these areas in the years to come. It is important that the objectives of the Storing are achieved, and that is a prerequisite for a good society. Finally, I would like to highlight the National Audit Office's international work and activities. It is good that the National Audit Office assists in the development of national audits in order to promote better and more effective governance in developing countries. The Committee welcomes the contribution of the National Audit Office to greater transparency and better governance of the international organisations of which Norway is a member. The Supervisory and Constitutional Committee notes that the international activities are in line with the Riksaudition's objective of being a leading actor in the development of public audits internationally.
ParlaMint-NO_2013-06-10.ana.ud446e5688	I would like to join the rapporteur's description that the Riksrevision is doing a good job. The National Audit Office’s vision is to contribute effectively to the control of the Storting and to promote good governance. However, when we look at what has been presented to us, that in 2012 the Riksaudition sent 15 management audit reports to the Stortinget, and these administrative audits document weak achievement, low efficiency and/or administrative practices in the areas under investigation, there is evidence that the experience gained so far from the management audit reports has not been sufficient to bring about the necessary improvement – so to speak, if we are to be aware that the areas that have been revised here are areas that one has reason to see that there is a greater need to do something about. What I want with this post is to draw attention to how the Norwegian government is being run today, how management is being carried out. We've been involved with this in relation to emergency services, we're quite often involved with it in specialist health services, and many have opinions about it in relation to Nav. Very many people who are affected by it have an opinion about it in relation to child protection, and no later than today we received a press release on how the administration is within the Railway Agency. I believe that this topic of management has far too little focus, and that there is too little debate about the reports that the Riksrevision is doing, which leaves the impression that there are great opportunities for improvement here. There was much discussion in the Committee's position on 22 July, one in Chapter 3.8.1 of the Government Parties' comments on what is known as "mission-based management", a new decentralised management philosophy in order to ensure initiative, creativity and ownership of the tasks, sense of responsibility and thus a collective commitment, the purpose of which is that the one with the best understanding of the situation should recognize the importance of, and be trained to act maritimely, but in line with, the overall level's intention, as it was at the time. Obviously, management needs to be exercised differently whether it is administrative bodies, where rule of law values have a high priority, or in emergency agencies, but the key word is to take responsibility, to have a management that is such that a person gives responsibility, and to uncover the persons standing in the first line. I think it's important that, based on the National Audit Office's management audit reports, we get more discussion about how we exercise governance in the public sector, so that we ensure that this responsibility gets better, and thus becomes a more effective, more legal certainty and better management of what is so essential to ensure justice and security for the citizens.

Alternatively, between the Committee's position and proposal No. 2 from the Progress Party and the Right. Proposal No. 2 sounds: ‘§ 30 Infringement fee shall be replaced by the following: An undertaking may be liable for an infringement fee if the undertaking or any person acting on its behalf infringes the provisions of or comply with the law. This applies even if the liability for the infringement cannot be directed to any individual. In determining whether the undertaking is liable to be charged an infringement fee and in measuring the fee, particular attention may be paid to: (a) the preventive effect of the infringement fee; (b) the gravity of the infringement; (c) whether the undertaking could have prevented the infringement by internal control, guidelines, instructions, training or other measures; (d) whether the infringement has been committed in order to promote the interests of the undertaking; (e) whether the undertaking has had or could have benefited from the infringement; (f) the financial capacity of the undertaking; (g) whether there is repetition; (h) the measures taken to prevent or remedy the effects of the infringement; and (i) whether the infringement is a risk of, or has led to serious or irreparable environmental damage. The amount of the infringement fee may not exceed 15 times the basic amount of the public interest [[G]]
